M=(1-A)(\mathbf{B}_{0}X+U)+A(\mathbf{B}_{1}X+U^{\prime})\,,

where
\mathbf{B}_{1},\mathbf{B}_{0}\in\mathbb{R}^{q\times p}
are row-sparse matrices, where each row of both
\mathbf{B}_{0}
and
\mathbf{B}_{1}
contains
s
(positions are randomly chosen) nonzero elements sampled from
\mathcal{U}(0.5,1.5)
.
The errors
U,U^{\prime}{\sim}\mathcal{N}(0,\Sigma_{U}),\Sigma_{U}=R_{U}\Lambda_{U}R_{U}^{\top}
, where
R_{U}\in\mathbb{R}^{q\times q}
is orthonormal and
\Lambda_{U}=\mathrm{diag}(\lambda_{1},\ldots,\lambda_{q})
with
1\leq\lambda_{\min}(\Sigma_{U})\leq\lambda_{\max}(\Sigma_{U})\leq 2
.

4.

Finally, we generate the response variable from the model:

Y=(1-A)(X^{\top}\beta_{0}+M^{\top}\gamma_{0}+\epsilon^{\prime})+A(X^{\top}\beta_{1}+M^{\top}\gamma_{1}+\epsilon)\,,

where
\beta_{0},\beta_{1}\in\mathbb{R}^{p}
are sparse vectors with
k_{\beta}
non-zero elements. The non-zero indices are chosen randomly, and the values corresponding to those indices are sampled from
\mathcal{U}(0.5,1.5)
. Similarly,
\gamma_{0},\gamma_{1}\in\mathbb{R}^{q}
are also sparse vectors with
k_{\gamma}
non-zero elements. As before, the non-zero indices are sampled randomly, and values corresponding to those indices are sampled from
\mathcal{U}(0.5,1.5)
, and
\epsilon,\epsilon^{\prime}\overset{i.i.d.}{\sim}\mathcal{N}(0,\sigma^{2})
.

With the above-mentioned data-generating process, we study four regimes:

•

Base.
The sparsity levels are fixed at
s=k_{\beta}=k_{\gamma}=3
across all dimensions, every nonzero coefficient is a moderate signal sampled from
\mathcal{U}(0.5,1.5)
, and the errors are homoskedastic,
\epsilon,\epsilon^{\prime}\sim\mathcal{N}(0,\sigma^{2})
.

•

Increasing sparsity.
The number of nonzero coefficients grows with the
ambient dimension, with
s=k_{\beta}=k_{\gamma}\in\{5,10,15,20\}
corresponding to

p+q\in\{100,800,1500,2500\}
; nonzero values are again moderate, sampled
from
\mathcal{U}(0.5,1.5)
. To keep the mediator model well-scaled as its
support grows, the rows of the mediator coefficient matrices
\mathbf{B}_{0},\mathbf{B}_{1}\in\mathbb{R}^{q\times p}
are normalized to unit
\ell_{1}
norm.

•

Vanishing minimum non-zero signal.
The sparsity is now fixed at
s=k_{\beta}=k_{\gamma}=3
, but approximately half of the nonzero coefficients of the regression coefficients are now generated from near the detection boundary, with magnitudes of order
n^{-1/2}_{\mathrm{max}}
(
n_{\max}=1250
, the maximum number of samples used in our simulation, and these coordinates are sampled from
\mathcal{U}(0.8,1.2)/\sqrt{n_{\max}}
), while the remaining coefficients are moderate as in the base case. This regime assesses the performance of the estimator when part of the signal is only weakly identifiable.

•

Heteroskedastic noise.
We retain the sparsity (
s=k_{\beta}=k_{\gamma}=3
) and moderate signals as in the base case, but replace the homoskedastic errors in item (4) by conditionally heteroskedastic ones whose variance is now a function of both the covariates and the mediators. Specifically, we generate errors as
\epsilon_{i}\mid(X_{i},M_{i})\sim\mathcal{N}\!\big(0,\,\sigma^{2}\,v(X_{i},M_{i})\big)
(analogously for
\epsilon^{\prime}_{i}
), where

v(X_{i},M_{i})\;=\;\frac{1+\tfrac{1}{2}\big[(X_{i}^{\top}a_{\epsilon})^{2}+(M_{i}^{\top}b_{\epsilon})^{2}\big]}{\;\mathbb{E}\big[\,1+\tfrac{1}{2}\big((X^{\top}a_{\epsilon})^{2}+(M^{\top}b_{\epsilon})^{2}\big)\big]\;}\,,

(6.1)

with sparse
a_{\epsilon}\in\mathbb{R}^{p},\ b_{\epsilon}\in\mathbb{R}^{q}
satisfying
\|a_{\epsilon}\|_{0}=\|b_{\epsilon}\|_{0}=3
and nonzero entries of
(a_{\epsilon},b_{\epsilon})
drawn from
\mathcal{U}(0,1)
. The normalization ensures
\mathbb{E}[v(X,M)]=1
, so the marginal noise level matches the homoskedastic case.

In Assumption
5.3
, we claim that
0<c_{\epsilon}<C_{\epsilon}<\infty
and
c_{\epsilon}\leq\nu_{a}(X,M)\leq C_{\epsilon}
almost surely, whereas in Equation (
6.1
),
v(X_{i},M_{i})
can potentially be unbounded. We include this setting as a stress test, allowing the conditional variance to be unbounded in order to assess the robustness of our estimator beyond the theoretical conditions. Our Assumption
5.3
could also be extended to accommodate such settings via an appropriate truncation that controls extreme conditional variances.

The number of samples
n
varies over
\{250,500,750,1000,1250\}
. For each value of
n
in this set, we vary
p+q
over the set
\{100,800,1500,2500\}
. Furthermore, for the homoskedastic case, the noise variance
\sigma^{2}\in\{0.1,1\}
. Recall that our parameter of interest is
\theta_{10}=\bar{X}^{\top}(\beta_{1}+\mathbf{B}_{0}^{\top}\gamma_{1})
where
\bar{X}
is the sample average of the covariates. We keep
X
and other parameters (e.g.,
(\beta_{a},\gamma_{a},\alpha,\mathbf{B}_{a})
for
a\in\{0,1\}
) fixed in the simulation study to keep the parameter of interest unchanged.

s=3,\ k_{1}=k_{2}=3

\sigma^{2}=1

Table 1:
Simulation results for the debiased (
\hat{\theta}_{10}^{\mathrm{cf}}
) and naive (
\hat{\theta}^{\rm plug-in}
) estimators under the base DGP with
\sigma^{2}=1
. Numbers are averaged over 500 replications with
K_{1}=K_{2}=2.75
.

For brevity, the main text reports only the base regime with
\sigma^{2}=1
(Table
1
). The remaining configurations, namely the base regime with
\sigma^{2}=0.1
, the increasing-sparsity, vanishing minimal non-zero signal, and the heteroskedastic-noise regimes, together with the sensitivity analyses, can be found in Section
F
of the supplementary material (Tables
7
–
11
and
12
–
14
).

We use Algorithm
1
to compute our estimator. The Lasso estimates in Step 2 and Step 4 are obtained using the R package
glmnet
, where the tuning parameter is selected by cross-validation using the
lambda.1se
rule from R function
cv.glmnet
(
Friedman et al. (2010)
). The debiasing weights in Step 1 and Step 3 are solved by solving the corresponding optimization problem via the R package
pogs
with
K_{1}=K_{2}=2.75
. This particular choice of
(K_{1},K_{2})
is motivated by our sensitivity analysis, as elaborated in Table
12
-
14
in Section
F
of the supplementary material. This choice remains consistent with previous studies (e.g.,
Javanmard and Montanari (2014)
). We also record the computational cost under a representative simulation setting.
Specifically, when
n=1000
and
p+q=1500
, running the
500
Monte Carlo replications across
30
CPU cores takes approximately
12
minutes of wall-clock time, which amortizes to about
1.4
seconds per replication (
12\times 60/500\approx 1.4
).

Figure 2:
Histogram and QQ plot of
\sqrt{n}(\hat{\theta}-\theta)
under
n=750,p+q=1500,\sigma_{\epsilon}=0.1
.

The simulation results are summarized in Table
1
and in Section
F
of supplementary materials. Each table reports the root-mean-squared error (RMSE) and standard deviation (SD) of both
\hat{\theta}^{\rm plug-in}
and
\hat{\theta}_{10}^{\rm cf}
, averaged over 500 Monte Carlo replications,
along with the estimated confidence-interval length and empirical coverage under both a Monte Carlo standard deviation and our estimated analytical standard error.

The results indicate that the debiased estimator
\hat{\theta}_{10}^{\rm cf}
consistently achieves a lower RMSE than the naive estimator, with the performance gap becoming increasingly prominent as the sample size
n
grows. Furthermore, the standard deviations of the two estimators are comparable, suggesting that the improved RMSE of our debiased estimator is attributable to the successful bias correction.
This pattern is consistent across all four regimes: in the base, minimal-signal, and heteroskedastic-noise regimes,
\hat{\theta}_{10}^{\rm cf}
achieves close-to-nominal coverage as
n
increases, and the estimated analytical standard error
\hat{\sigma}_{n}^{\rm cf}
closely matches the Monte Carlo standard deviation. In the increasing-sparsity regime, the same conclusions hold in moderate dimensions, while the most challenging high-dimensional, small-sample cells (
p+q=2500
,
n=250
) are, as expected, the hardest, with
\hat{\theta}_{10}^{\rm cf}
nonetheless remaining more accurate and better calibrated than
\hat{\theta}^{\rm plug-in}
.

To further illustrate the performance of the estimators, we display histograms of the naive (red) and debiased (blue) estimators in the left column of Figure
2
, based on 500 Monte Carlo replications. The estimators are centered around the true parameter and scaled by
\sqrt{n}
, where
n
is the number of samples.
The histogram clearly reveals that the naive estimator is substantially biased, while the debiased estimator is centered around zero as a result of successful bias correction. Additionally, the Q-Q plot of the debiased estimator, shown in the right column of Figure
2
, aligns closely with the standard normal distribution, providing empirical evidence for its asymptotic normality and supporting our theoretical results.

Remark 6.1

(Comparison with
Guo et al. (2022)
)
.

We also conducted additional numerical experiments to compare the performance of our proposed estimator with the estimator of
Guo et al. (2022)
in a simplified setting where both methods can be applied.
A direct comparison is not possible, since the framework of
Guo et al. (2022)
;
Guo et al. (2023)
;
Guo et al. (2024)
does not accommodate treatment-covariate interactions in the mediator model or treatment–covariate/treatment–mediator interactions in the outcome model. Therefore, in the supplementary material, we consider a simplified common setting: we remove the treatment interactions from our model and include UHD covariates with a natural modification of their procedure. The details can be found in Section
D.1
of the supplementary document. It is evident from the results that, especially when the sparsity level is high or the minimum active signal strength is small, our debiased estimator outperforms the modified estimator in
Guo et al. (2022)
in both RMSE and asymptotic coverage.

6.2
Real Data Experiments

DNA methylation, an epigenetic modification characterized by the addition of a methyl group to the DNA molecule, is essential for regulating gene expression and is linked to various biological functions, and is often regarded as a potential mediator linking exposures to health outcomes
(
Fujii et al., 2022
)
. Recent studies suggest that smoking leads to widespread DNA methylation changes, which may contribute to cancer development and progression
(
Tan et al., 2013
;
Zhang et al., 2016
)
. Meanwhile, certain smoking-associated methylation markers have been linked to increased mortality risk among lung cancer patients
(
Zhang et al., 2016
;
Mehta et al., 2015
)
.
As an application of our developed methodology, we apply our method to the data from the lung cancer cohort of The Cancer Genome Atlas (TCGA) project to study the direct and indirect effects of smoking on the survival time of patients diagnosed with lung squamous cell carcinoma and lung adenocarcinoma. The data is publicly available at (
https://xenabrowser.net/datapages/
).
The dataset includes DNA methylation data from 907 individuals, feature data for 1,299 individuals, and survival data for 1,145 individuals, among which
833
individuals were common.

•

Our response variable,
Y
, is the logarithm of the survival time, where the survival time is measured as the number of days from diagnosis to death or the last follow-up date.
We used the inter-quartile range to detect and remove the outliers in the response variable.

•

We selected
20
clinically relevant features,
X
(e.g., age, sex, etc.; see full list in Section
E
of the supplementary material) for our study by excluding all those features which has more than
100
missing values. See the Supplementary Materials for the complete list of the chosen features. We then imputed the remaining missing values; for numerical features (11), we used
softImpute

(
Mazumder et al., 2010
)
and for categorical variables (9), we used
mice

(
Van Buuren and Groothuis-Oudshoorn, 2011
)
imputation.
Furthermore, we considered up to third-order polynomial interactions for better expressiveness. These operations ultimately yielded a
42
-dimensional covariate.

•

The DNA methylation data comprises
350,019
DNA methylation marker values (
M
), represented as continuous ratios ranging from 0 to 1, reflecting the intensity of methylation, where higher values indicate a greater degree of methylation. For more details, see (
https://www.cancer.gov/ccg/research/genome-sequencing/tcga
). We selected only those mediators with no missing values. This reduces the number of mediators to
264,797
.

After the data pre-processing, we are left with
n=738
units, each with
42
features
(X)
and
264,797
mediators (
M
). The treatment indicator (
A
) is the smoking status of the units (
A=1
indicates a smoker).
Among these samples, there are
42.6\%(315)
females and
57.3\%(423)
males.
The patients’ ages range from 33 to 90 years, with a median age of 67 years. The median survival time is 603 days (1.65 years).

To estimate the direct and indirect effects (NDE and NIE) of smoking on the survival time of lung cancer patients, we fit the following high-dimensional linear model to the pre-processed data:

\displaystyle M_{i}

\displaystyle=(1-A_{i})(\mathbf{B}_{0}X_{i}+U_{i})+A_{i}(\mathbf{B}_{1}X_{i}+U^{\prime}_{i}),\hskip 14.45377pti=1,\ldots,738,

\displaystyle Y_{i}

\displaystyle=(1-A_{i})(X_{i}^{\top}\beta_{0}+M_{i}^{\top}\gamma_{0}+\epsilon_{i}^{\prime})+A_{i}(X_{i}^{\top}\beta_{1}+M_{i}^{\top}\gamma_{1}+\epsilon_{i})\,.

(6.2)

We then apply Algorithm
2
and
3
to estimate the NDE, NIE, and total treatment effect. For our real data application, one full run of the proposed algorithm (one bootstrap replicate) takes approximately
48
seconds using
10
CPU cores.

Table
2
summarizes the estimates of the NDE, NIE, and total effect of smoking on log survival time. The estimate of the total effect implies that, on average, smokers have
92.14%
(
e^{-0.0819}\times 100\%
) of the survival time of non-smokers, i.e., smoking reduces approximately
7.86%
of the survival time of lung cancer patients. Of this total effect, our results demonstrate that approximately
5.59%

=100\times(1-e^{-0.0575})\%
corresponds to the indirect effect (NIE), i.e., the impact of smoking on survival time mediated via DNA methylation. In comparison, approximately
2.41%

=100\times(1-e^{-0.0244})\%
corresponds to the direct effect (NDE), which can be attributed to pathways beyond the methylation mechanism considered in this analysis.

Since the confidence interval for the indirect effect estimate excludes zero, while the confidence interval for the direct effect estimate contains zero, our findings suggest that the estimated effect of smoking on survival time is primarily mediated through the DNA methylation mechanism captured in this analysis. This indicates that DNA methylation plays an important mediating role in the current dataset, although additional methylation mechanisms and other biological pathways may remain to be investigated.

Table 2:
Summary of the estimated effects, bootstrap means, and 95% quantile confidence intervals based on 300 bootstrap samples.

We compared the results with a fixed-dimensional OLS-based approach. Note that if both
X
and
M
are low-dimensional in Equation (
6.2
), we can easily estimate the NDE, NIE (and consequently the total effect) by computing ordinary least squared (OLS) estimators via regressing i)
Y
on
(X,M)
(on treatment and control observations separately), and ii)
M
on
X
(on treatment and control observations separately). To make the problem low-dimensional, we selected CpG sites (cg19757631, cg05147638, cg24720672, cg08108679, cg05575921, cg24859433) which were identified as significant mediators in previous studies
(
Cui et al., 2021
;
Zhang et al., 2021a
;
Zhang et al., 2021b
)
, as our
M
, and
20
clinically relevant features as our
X
(i.e., we do not consider their higher-order terms and interactions).

In Table
3
, we present the results in a fixed-dimensional setting. The results reveal that the OLS-based approach produces conclusions about the indirect and total effects that are contrary to intuition. The estimate of the indirect and total effect imply that smokers have
128.80\%
(
e^{0.2531}\times 100\%
) (mediated through DNA methylation), and
126.52\%
(
e^{0.2353}\times 100\%
) of survival time of non-smokers, i.e., smoking increases
\approx 29\%

=100\times(1-e^{0.2531})\%
(corresponds to the NIE) and
\approx 27\%

=100\times(1-e^{0.2353})\%
of the survival time of lung cancer patients. Furthermore, the confidence interval for the direct effect and total effect includes zero, suggesting a lack of statistical significance. These findings further emphasize the limitations of low-dimensional mediator selection and conventional modeling approaches, reinforcing the importance of incorporating high-dimensional methodologies for accurate mediation analysis in complex biological systems.

Table 3:
Summary of the effects in OLS (CpG sites selected from prior studies) with mean and confidence interval (CI) under 300-sample bootstrap.

7
Conclusion

In this paper, we introduced a novel methodology for estimating the mediation functional and, consequently, of the NDE, NIE in the presence of ultra-high-dimensional covariates and mediators. Our model is sufficiently flexible to accommodate interactions between the treatment assignment and both covariates and mediators in the conditional mean of the response, as well as interactions between the treatment assignment and covariates in the conditional expectation of the mediators. The primary contribution is to develop a novel debiasing framework to obtain a
\sqrt{n}
-CAN estimate of the mediation functional and, consequently, of the NDE, NIE in the presence of these challenges, which is potentially applicable to other problems sharing a similar structural equation model. To the best of our knowledge, this is the first study to address the challenge of estimating NDE and NIE when dealing with high-dimensional covariates, mediators, and their interactions with the treatment variable simultaneously. While this paper focuses on a linear model, extending the methodology to generalized linear models is a natural next step, which we leave as an intriguing direction for future research.

Appendix A
Proof of Proposition
4.1

Proof.

We first consider
\hat{\theta}_{10}^{1\to 2}
. Recall that
\theta_{10}=\bar{X}^{\top}(\beta_{1}+\mathbf{B}_{0}^{\top}\gamma_{1}),\ \phi_{1}^{\top}=(\beta_{1}^{\top}\ \ \gamma_{1}^{\top}).

We also recall the definitions of
\hat{\vartheta}_{1}
and

\hat{\vartheta}_{2}
in Algorithm
1
. Adding and
subtracting
(a^{(1)})^{\top}\phi_{1}
in
\hat{\vartheta}_{1}
, we have

\displaystyle\hat{\vartheta}_{1}

\displaystyle=\hat{\vartheta}_{1}-(a^{(1)})^{\top}\phi_{1}+(a^{(1)})^{\top}\phi_{1}

\displaystyle=\left(a^{(1)}-\mathbf{W}_{t,2}^{\top}\hat{\tau}_{1}^{(2)}\right)^{\top}\left(\hat{\phi}^{(2)}-\phi_{1}\right)+(a^{(1)})^{\top}\phi_{1}+(\hat{\tau}_{1}^{(2)})^{\top}{\bm{\epsilon}}_{t}^{(2)}.

Similarly, adding and subtracting
\bar{X}^{\top}b^{(2)}
in

\hat{\vartheta}_{2}
gives

\displaystyle\hat{\vartheta}_{2}

\displaystyle=\hat{\vartheta}_{2}-\bar{X}^{\top}b^{(2)}+\bar{X}^{\top}b^{(2)}

\displaystyle=\left(\bar{X}-\mathbf{X}_{c,1}^{\top}\hat{\tau}_{2}^{(1)}\right)^{\top}\left(\hat{b}^{(1)}-b^{(2)}\right)+\bar{X}^{\top}b^{(2)}+(\hat{\tau}_{2}^{(1)})^{\top}\mathbf{U}_{c}^{(1)}\hat{\gamma}_{1}^{(2)}.

Therefore,

\displaystyle\hat{\theta}_{10}^{1\to 2}

\displaystyle=\hat{\vartheta}_{1}+\hat{\vartheta}_{2}-\hat{m}_{c,1}^{\top}\hat{\gamma}_{1}^{(2)}

\displaystyle=\left(a^{(1)}-\mathbf{W}_{t,2}^{\top}\hat{\tau}_{1}^{(2)}\right)^{\top}\left(\hat{\phi}^{(2)}-\phi_{1}\right)+\left(\bar{X}-\mathbf{X}_{c,1}^{\top}\hat{\tau}_{2}^{(1)}\right)^{\top}\left(\hat{b}^{(1)}-b^{(2)}\right)

\displaystyle\hskip 9.24994pt+(a^{(1)})^{\top}\phi_{1}+\bar{X}^{\top}b^{(2)}+(\hat{\tau}_{2}^{(1)})^{\top}\mathbf{U}_{c}^{(1)}\left(\hat{\gamma}_{1}^{(2)}-\gamma_{1}\right)-\hat{m}_{c,1}^{\top}\hat{\gamma}_{1}^{(2)}

\displaystyle\hskip 9.24994pt+(\hat{\tau}_{1}^{(2)})^{\top}{\bm{\epsilon}}_{t}^{(2)}+(\hat{\tau}_{2}^{(1)})^{\top}\mathbf{U}_{c}^{(1)}\gamma_{1}.

(A.1)

Next, note that
\hat{m}_{c,1}=\mathbf{M}_{c,1}^{\top}\hat{\tau}_{2}^{(1)}=\mathbf{B}_{0}\mathbf{X}_{c,1}^{\top}\hat{\tau}_{2}^{(1)}+(\mathbf{U}_{c}^{(1)})^{\top}\hat{\tau}_{2}^{(1)}.

Moreover,
(a^{(1)})^{\top}\phi_{1}=\bar{X}^{\top}\beta_{1}+\hat{m}_{c,1}^{\top}\gamma_{1},\ \bar{X}^{\top}b^{(2)}=\bar{X}^{\top}\mathbf{B}_{0}^{\top}\hat{\gamma}_{1}^{(2)}.

The second line in (
A.1
) follows that

\displaystyle(a^{(1)})^{\top}\phi_{1}+\bar{X}^{\top}b^{(2)}+(\hat{\tau}_{2}^{(1)})^{\top}\mathbf{U}_{c}^{(1)}\left(\hat{\gamma}_{1}^{(2)}-\gamma_{1}\right)-\hat{m}_{c,1}^{\top}\hat{\gamma}_{1}^{(2)}

\displaystyle=\bar{X}^{\top}\beta_{1}+\hat{m}_{c,1}^{\top}\gamma_{1}+\bar{X}^{\top}\mathbf{B}_{0}^{\top}\hat{\gamma}_{1}^{(2)}+(\hat{\tau}_{2}^{(1)})^{\top}\mathbf{U}_{c}^{(1)}\left(\hat{\gamma}_{1}^{(2)}-\gamma_{1}\right)-\hat{m}_{c,1}^{\top}\hat{\gamma}_{1}^{(2)}

\displaystyle=\bar{X}^{\top}\beta_{1}+\bar{X}^{\top}\mathbf{B}_{0}^{\top}\hat{\gamma}_{1}^{(2)}+\left[\hat{m}_{c,1}-(\mathbf{U}_{c}^{(1)})^{\top}\hat{\tau}_{2}^{(1)}\right]^{\top}\left(\gamma_{1}-\hat{\gamma}_{1}^{(2)}\right)

\displaystyle=\bar{X}^{\top}\beta_{1}+\bar{X}^{\top}\mathbf{B}_{0}^{\top}\hat{\gamma}_{1}^{(2)}+\left(\mathbf{B}_{0}\mathbf{X}_{c,1}^{\top}\hat{\tau}_{2}^{(1)}\right)^{\top}\left(\gamma_{1}-\hat{\gamma}_{1}^{(2)}\right)

\displaystyle=\bar{X}^{\top}\left(\beta_{1}+\mathbf{B}_{0}^{\top}\gamma_{1}\right)+\left(\bar{X}-\mathbf{X}_{c,1}^{\top}\hat{\tau}_{2}^{(1)}\right)^{\top}\mathbf{B}_{0}^{\top}\left(\hat{\gamma}_{1}^{(2)}-\gamma_{1}\right)

\displaystyle=\theta_{10}+\left(\bar{X}-\mathbf{X}_{c,1}^{\top}\hat{\tau}_{2}^{(1)}\right)^{\top}\mathbf{B}_{0}^{\top}\left(\hat{\gamma}_{1}^{(2)}-\gamma_{1}\right).

Substituting this identity into (
A.1
), we obtain

\displaystyle\hat{\theta}_{10}^{1\to 2}-\theta_{10}

\displaystyle=\left(a^{(1)}-\mathbf{W}_{t,2}^{\top}\hat{\tau}_{1}^{(2)}\right)^{\top}\left(\hat{\phi}^{(2)}-\phi_{1}\right)

\displaystyle\hskip 9.24994pt+\left(\bar{X}-\mathbf{X}_{c,1}^{\top}\hat{\tau}_{2}^{(1)}\right)^{\top}\left(\hat{b}^{(1)}-b^{(2)}\right)

\displaystyle\hskip 9.24994pt+\left(\bar{X}-\mathbf{X}_{c,1}^{\top}\hat{\tau}_{2}^{(1)}\right)^{\top}\mathbf{B}_{0}^{\top}\left(\hat{\gamma}_{1}^{(2)}-\gamma_{1}\right)

\displaystyle\hskip 9.24994pt+(\hat{\tau}_{1}^{(2)})^{\top}{\bm{\epsilon}}_{t}^{(2)}+(\hat{\tau}_{2}^{(1)})^{\top}\mathbf{U}_{c}^{(1)}\gamma_{1}

\displaystyle=:\Delta_{n}^{(1)}+V_{n}^{(1)}.

By the same argument with the roles of
\mathcal{D}_{1}
and
\mathcal{D}_{2}
reversed, we have

\displaystyle\hat{\theta}_{10}^{2\to 1}-\theta_{10}

\displaystyle=\left(a^{(2)}-\mathbf{W}_{t,1}^{\top}\hat{\tau}_{1}^{(1)}\right)^{\top}\left(\hat{\phi}^{(1)}-\phi_{1}\right)

\displaystyle\hskip 9.24994pt+\left(\bar{X}-\mathbf{X}_{c,2}^{\top}\hat{\tau}_{2}^{(2)}\right)^{\top}\left(\hat{b}^{(2)}-b^{(1)}\right)

\displaystyle\hskip 9.24994pt+\left(\bar{X}-\mathbf{X}_{c,2}^{\top}\hat{\tau}_{2}^{(2)}\right)^{\top}\mathbf{B}_{0}^{\top}\left(\hat{\gamma}_{1}^{(1)}-\gamma_{1}\right)

\displaystyle\hskip 9.24994pt+(\hat{\tau}_{1}^{(1)})^{\top}{\bm{\epsilon}}_{t}^{(1)}+(\hat{\tau}_{2}^{(2)})^{\top}\mathbf{U}_{c}^{(2)}\gamma_{1}

\displaystyle=:\Delta_{n}^{(2)}+V_{n}^{(2)}.

Letting
j^{\prime}=3-j
, for
j\in\{1,2\}
,

\displaystyle\Delta_{n}^{(j)}

\displaystyle=\left(a^{(j)}-\mathbf{W}_{t,j^{\prime}}^{\top}\hat{\tau}_{1}^{(j^{\prime})}\right)^{\top}\left(\hat{\phi}^{(j^{\prime})}-\phi_{1}\right)

\displaystyle\hskip 9.24994pt+\left(\bar{X}-\mathbf{X}_{c,j}^{\top}\hat{\tau}_{2}^{(j)}\right)^{\top}\left(\hat{b}^{(j)}-b^{(j^{\prime})}\right)

\displaystyle\hskip 9.24994pt+\left(\bar{X}-\mathbf{X}_{c,j}^{\top}\hat{\tau}_{2}^{(j)}\right)^{\top}\mathbf{B}_{0}^{\top}\left(\hat{\gamma}_{1}^{(j^{\prime})}-\gamma_{1}\right),

which gives (
4.1
). Since
\hat{\theta}_{10}^{\rm cf}=\frac{1}{2}\left(\hat{\theta}_{10}^{1\to 2}+\hat{\theta}_{10}^{2\to 1}\right),

we have

\displaystyle\hat{\theta}_{10}^{\rm cf}-\theta_{10}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\left[\left(\hat{\theta}_{10}^{1\to 2}-\theta_{10}\right)+\left(\hat{\theta}_{10}^{2\to 1}-\theta_{10}\right)\right]

\displaystyle=\frac{\Delta_{n}^{(1)}+\Delta_{n}^{(2)}}{2}+\frac{V_{n}^{(1)}+V_{n}^{(2)}}{2}

\displaystyle=\Delta_{n}^{\rm cf}+V_{n}^{\rm cf}.

Moreover, by the definitions of the fold-specific error,
(\hat{\tau}_{1}^{(j)})^{\top}{\bm{\epsilon}}_{t}^{(j)}=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}i\in\mathcal{D}_{j}\\
A_{i}=1\end{subarray}}\hat{\tau}_{1,i}^{(j)}{\epsilon}_{i},\ (\hat{\tau}_{2}^{(j)})^{\top}\mathbf{U}_{c}^{(j)}\gamma_{1}=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}i\in\mathcal{D}_{j}\\
A_{i}=0\end{subarray}}\hat{\tau}_{2,i}^{(j)}U_{i}^{\top}\gamma_{1}.

we obtain,

V_{n}^{\rm cf}=\frac{1}{2}\sum_{j=1}^{2}\left[\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}i\in\mathcal{D}_{j}\\
A_{i}=1\end{subarray}}\hat{\tau}_{1,i}^{(j)}{\epsilon}_{i}+\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}i\in\mathcal{D}_{j}\\
A_{i}=0\end{subarray}}\hat{\tau}_{2,i}^{(j)}U_{i}^{\top}\gamma_{1}\right].

The bound in (
4.2
) can be proved directly via applying Hölder’s inequality to each of the three terms, yielding

\displaystyle|\Delta_{n}^{(j)}|

\displaystyle\leq\left\|\mathbf{B}_{0}\left(\bar{X}-\mathbf{X}_{c,j}^{\top}\hat{\tau}_{2}^{(j)}\right)\right\|_{\infty}\left\|\hat{\gamma}_{1}^{(j^{\prime})}-\gamma_{1}\right\|_{1}

\displaystyle\hskip 9.24994pt+\left\|a^{(j)}-\mathbf{W}_{t,j^{\prime}}^{\top}\hat{\tau}_{1}^{(j^{\prime})}\right\|_{\infty}\left\|\hat{\phi}^{(j^{\prime})}-\phi_{1}\right\|_{1}

\displaystyle\hskip 9.24994pt+\left\|\bar{X}-\mathbf{X}_{c,j}^{\top}\hat{\tau}_{2}^{(j)}\right\|_{\infty}\left\|\hat{b}^{(j)}-b^{(j^{\prime})}\right\|_{1},

which proves (
4.2
). This completes the proof.
∎

Appendix B
Proof of Results from Section
5

B.1
Proof of Lemma
5.8

Proof.

Let us consider the optimization problem Equation (
3.1
) on
\mathcal{D}_{1}
, and the proof for
\mathcal{D}_{2}
is the same. To establish the feasibility, we will construct a vector
\tau_{2}^{0}
which satisfies the constraints in Equation (
3.1
). Towards that end, define:

\textstyle\tau^{0}_{2,i}=\frac{1}{n_{c,1}}\mu_{X}^{\top}\Sigma_{X,0}^{-1}(X_{i}-\mu_{X,0}),\hskip 18.49988pti\in\mathcal{D}_{1}\text{ with }A_{i}=0\,.

Here
\Sigma_{X,0}={\sf var}(X\mid A=0)
and
\mu_{X,0}=\mathbb{E}[X\mid A=0]
. From Assumption
5.3
we have
\mu_{X}^{\top}\Sigma_{X,0}^{-1}\mu_{X}<\infty
, which, along with the fact that
\lambda_{\min}(\Sigma_{X,0})\geq c_{X}>0
implies
\mu_{X}^{\top}\Sigma_{X,0}^{-2}\mu_{X}\leq C
for some constant
C
. Furthermore, by Assumption
5.3
,
\zeta_{i}=\mu_{X}^{\top}\Sigma_{X,0}^{-1}(X_{i}-\mu_{X,0})
is a centered sub-Gaussian random variable with sub-Gaussian constant
\mu_{X}^{\top}\Sigma_{X,0}^{-2}\mu_{X}
. Therefore, from the standard sub-Gaussian inequality, we have with probability going to 1:

\textstyle\max_{i}|\zeta_{i}|\leq C_{1}\sqrt{\log{n_{c,1}}}\implies\|\tau_{2}^{0}\|_{\infty}\leq C\frac{\sqrt{\log{n_{c,1}}}}{n_{c,1}}\leq n_{c,1}^{-\alpha}

for any
\alpha\in(0,1)
and for all large
n
. For the other constraint:

\displaystyle\|\bar{X}-\mathbf{X}_{c,1}^{\top}\tau_{2}^{0}\|_{\infty}

\displaystyle=\left\|\bar{X}-\frac{1}{n_{c,1}}\mathbf{X}_{c,1}^{\top}(\mathbf{X}_{c,1}-\mathbf{1}_{n_{c,1}}\mu_{X,0}^{\top})\Sigma_{X,0}^{-1}\mu_{X}\right\|_{\infty}

\displaystyle\leq\|\bar{X}-\mu_{X}\|_{\infty}+\left\|\mu_{X}-\frac{1}{n_{c,1}}\mathbf{X}_{c,1}^{\top}(\mathbf{X}_{c,1}-\mathbf{1}_{n_{c,1}}\mu_{X,0}^{\top})\Sigma_{X,0}^{-1}\mu_{X}\right\|_{\infty}

The first term on the right-hand side is again
\leq C\sqrt{\log{p}/n}
with probability going to
1
via simple sub-Gaussian concentration. Now we establish a bound on the second term on the right-hand-side of the above equation.
For notational simplicity set
v=\Sigma_{X,0}^{-1}\mu_{X}
and
Z_{i}=X_{i}-\mu_{X,0}
. Then

\textstyle\frac{1}{n_{c,1}}\mathbf{X}_{c}^{\top}\left(\mathbf{X}_{c}-\mathbf{1}_{n_{c,1}}\mu_{X,0}^{\top}\right)\Sigma_{X,0}^{-1}\mu_{X}=\frac{1}{n_{c,1}}\sum_{i=1}^{n_{c,1}}X_{i}Z_{i}^{\top}v.

Furthermore, we have:

\textstyle\mathbb{E}[X_{i}Z_{i}^{\top}\mid A_{i}=0]=\mathbb{E}[(\mu_{X,0}+Z_{i})Z_{i}^{\top}\mid A_{i}=0]=\Sigma_{X,0}

and consequently
\mathbb{E}[X_{i}Z_{i}^{\top}v\mid A=0]=\mu_{X}
. Therefore, defining
\tilde{Z}_{i}=X_{i}Z_{i}^{\top}v-\mu_{X}
, we have:

\textstyle\frac{1}{n_{c,1}}\mathbf{X}_{c}^{\top}\left(\mathbf{X}_{c}-\mathbf{1}_{n_{c,1}}\mu_{X,0}^{\top}\right)\Sigma_{X,0}^{-1}\mu_{X}-\mu_{X}=\frac{1}{n_{c,1}}\sum_{i=1}^{n_{c,1}}\tilde{Z}_{i},

It is immediate that
\tilde{Z}_{1},\cdots,\tilde{Z}_{n_{c,1}}
are
p
-dimensional independent centered random vectors conditional on the treatment indicators. Next, we argue that
\tilde{Z}_{i}
is a sub-exponential random vector. Towards that end, define the whitened covariate vector
P_{i}:=\Sigma_{X,0}^{-1/2}(X_{i}-\mu_{X,0})
for
1\leq i\leq n_{c,1}
and also define
z=\Sigma_{X,0}^{-1/2}\mu_{X}
and
u_{j}=\Sigma_{X,0}^{1/2}e_{j}
for
1\leq j\leq p
. Then, we have
Z_{i}=X_{i}-\mu_{X,0}=\Sigma_{X,0}^{1/2}P_{i}
which further implies
Z_{i}^{\top}v=P_{i}^{\top}z
and
Z_{ij}=P_{i}^{\top}u_{j}
for
1\leq j\leq p
. As a consequence,

\textstyle X_{ij}Z_{i}^{\top}v=\left(\mu_{X,0,j}+P_{i}^{\top}u_{j}\right)(P_{i}^{\top}z).

Moreover, since
\mathbb{E}[P_{i}P_{i}^{\top}\mid A_{i}=0]=I_{p}
, we also have:

\textstyle\mathbb{E}[(P_{i}^{\top}u_{j})(P_{i}^{\top}z)\mid A_{i}=0]=u_{j}^{\top}z=e_{j}^{\top}\mu_{X}=\mu_{X,j}.

Therefore,

\textstyle\tilde{Z}_{ij}=\mu_{X,0,j}P_{i}^{\top}z+(P_{i}^{\top}u_{j})(P_{i}^{\top}z)-\mathbb{E}[(P_{i}^{\top}u_{j})(P_{i}^{\top}z)\mid A_{i}=0].

(B.1)

Let
K_{X}<\infty
denote the uniform conditional sub-Gaussian norm bound for
X-\mu_{X,a}
in Assumption
5.3
. Since
\lambda_{\min}(\Sigma_{X,0})\geq c_{X}
, the vectors
P_{i}
are uniformly conditionally sub-Gaussian with

\textstyle\|P_{i}\|_{\psi_{2}\mid A_{i}=0}\leq K_{X}/\sqrt{c_{X}}=:K_{P}.

Furthermore, Assumption
5.3
implies
\|u_{j}\|_{2}^{2}\leq e_{j}^{\top}\Sigma_{X,0}e_{j}\leq C_{X}
, and
\|z\|_{2}^{2}=\mu_{X}^{\top}\Sigma_{X,0}^{-1}\mu_{X}\leq C_{\mu,\Sigma}
. Therefore,

\textstyle\|P_{i}^{\top}u_{j}\|_{\psi_{2}\mid A_{i}=0}\leq K_{P}\sqrt{C_{X}},\hskip 18.49988pt\|P_{i}^{\top}z\|_{\psi_{2}\mid A_{i}=0}\leq K_{P}\sqrt{C_{\mu,\Sigma}}.

Since the product of two sub-Gaussian random variables is sub-exponential, we have:

\textstyle\|(P_{i}^{\top}u_{j})(P_{i}^{\top}z)\|_{\psi_{1}\mid A_{i}=0}\leq CK_{P}^{2}\sqrt{C_{X}C_{\mu,\Sigma}}.

Also, since
|\mu_{X,0,j}|\leq\|\mu_{X,0}\|_{\infty}\leq C_{\mu}
, we have

\textstyle\|\mu_{X,0,j}P_{i}^{\top}z\|_{\psi_{1}\mid A_{i}=0}\leq CC_{\mu}K_{P}\sqrt{C_{\mu,\Sigma}}.

Using the standard centering inequality
\|Y-\mathbb{E}[Y]\|_{\psi_{1}}\leq C\|Y\|_{\psi_{1}}
, we conclude that

\textstyle\max_{1\leq j\leq p}\|\widetilde{Z}_{ij}\|_{\psi_{1}\mid A_{i}=0}\leq C\left\{C_{\mu}K_{P}\sqrt{C_{\mu,\Sigma}}+K_{P}^{2}\sqrt{C_{X}C_{\mu,\Sigma}}\right\}=:L_{X}.

As
L_{X}
does not depend on
j
, we have
\max_{1\leq j\leq p}\|\tilde{Z}_{ij}\|_{\psi_{1}}\leq L_{X}
, and consequently an application of Bernstein’s inequality yields, for
t>0
,

\textstyle\mathbb{P}\left(\left|\frac{1}{n_{c,1}}\sum_{i=1}^{n_{c,1}}\tilde{Z}_{ij}\right|>t\right)\leq 2{\sf exp}\left[-cn_{c,1}\min\left\{\frac{t^{2}}{L_{X}^{2}},\frac{t}{L_{X}}\right\}\right].

Taking a union bound over
j=1,\ldots,p
yields

\textstyle\mathbb{P}\left(\left\|\frac{1}{n_{c,1}}\sum_{i=1}^{n_{c,1}}\tilde{Z}_{i}\right\|_{\infty}>t\right)\leq 2{\sf exp}\left[\log{p}-cn_{c,1}\min\left\{\frac{t^{2}}{L_{X}^{2}},\frac{t}{L_{X}}\right\}\right]

Finally, if we choose
t=AL_{X}\sqrt{\log{p}/n_{c,1}}
for some sufficiently large constant
A>0
, we have:

\textstyle\mathbb{P}\left(\left\|\frac{1}{n_{c,1}}\sum_{i=1}^{n_{c,1}}\tilde{Z}_{i}\right\|_{\infty}\leq AL\sqrt{\frac{\log{p}}{n_{c,1}}}\right)\longrightarrow 1,\hskip 9.24994pt\text{as }n\uparrow\infty\,.

This immediately implies, with probability going to
1
:

\textstyle\left\|\mu_{X}-\frac{1}{n_{c,1}}\mathbf{X}_{c,1}^{\top}(\mathbf{X}_{c,1}-\mathbf{1}_{n_{c,1}}\mu_{X,0}^{\top})\Sigma_{X,0}^{-1}\mu_{X}\right\|_{\infty}\leq AL_{X}\sqrt{\frac{\log{p}}{n_{c,1}}}\,.

As a consequence,
\tau_{2}^{0}
is a feasible solution and consequently the optimization problem in Equation (
3.1
) is feasible. Furthermore, from the optimality of
\hat{\tau}_{2}^{(1)}
, we have
n\|\hat{\tau}_{2}^{(1)}\|_{2}^{2}\leq n\|\tau_{2}^{0}\|_{2}^{2}
. On the other hand, from the definition,

\displaystyle n\|\tau_{2}^{0}\|_{2}^{2}

\displaystyle=\frac{n}{n_{c,1}}\frac{1}{n_{c,1}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}i\in\mathcal{D}_{1}\\
A_{i}=0\end{subarray}}v^{\top}(X_{i}-\mu_{X,0})(X_{i}-\mu_{X,0})^{\top}v

\displaystyle=\frac{nn_{1}}{n_{c,1}^{2}}\frac{1}{n_{1}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}i\in\mathcal{D}_{1}\end{subarray}}v^{\top}(X_{i}-\mu_{X,0})(X_{i}-\mu_{X,0})^{\top}v\mathds{1}_{A_{i}=0}=\frac{n}{n_{c,1}}v^{\top}\hat{\Sigma}^{(1)}_{X,0}v

Now,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\frac{1}{n_{1}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}i\in\mathcal{D}_{1}\end{subarray}}(X_{i}-\mu_{X,0})(X_{i}-\mu_{X,0})^{\top}\mathds{1}_{A_{i}=0}\right]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\left[(X-\mu_{X,0})(X-\mu_{X,0})^{\top}\mathds{1}_{A=0}\right]=\Sigma_{X,0}\mathbb{P}(A=0)

As
\Sigma_{X,0}
has bounded maximum eigenvalue and
\|v\|_{2}^{2}
is bounded,

\textstyle\mathbb{E}\left[\frac{1}{n_{1}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}i\in\mathcal{D}_{1}\end{subarray}}v^{\top}(X_{i}-\mu_{X,0})(X_{i}-\mu_{X,0})^{\top}v\mathds{1}_{A_{i}=0}\right]\leq C\,.

Therefore, an application of Markov inequality yields:

\textstyle\frac{1}{n_{1}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}i\in\mathcal{D}_{1}\end{subarray}}v^{\top}(X_{i}-\mu_{X,0})(X_{i}-\mu_{X,0})^{\top}v\mathds{1}_{A_{i}=0}=\frac{n}{n_{c,1}}v^{\top}\hat{\Sigma}^{(1)}_{X,0}v=O_{p}(1)

This, along with the fact that
n\sim n_{1}\sim n_{c,1}
(as
\mathbb{P}(A=1)
is bounded away from
0
and
1
and each split has half of the samples), yields the conclusion.

Next, we will establish the feasibility of the optimization problem Equation (
3.2
). Towards that end recall the definition of
\eta^{*}
and
a^{*}
from part (3) of Assumption
5.3
;
a^{*}=(\mu_{X}^{\top}\ \ (\mathbf{B}_{0}\mu_{X})^{\top})^{\top}
and
\eta^{*}=\Omega_{W,1}^{-1}a^{*}
, where
\Omega_{W,1}=\mathbb{E}[WW^{\top}\mid A=1]
. Define a vector
\tau_{1}^{0}
as:

\textstyle\tau^{0}_{1,i}=\frac{1}{n_{t,2}}W_{i}^{\top}\eta^{*},\hskip 18.49988pti\in\mathcal{D}_{2},\text{ with }A_{i}=1\,.

Therefore, we have
\tau_{1}^{0}=(\mathbf{W}_{t,2}\eta^{*})/n_{t,2}
. Hence, we have:

\textstyle a-\mathbf{W}_{t,2}^{\top}\tau_{1}^{0}=a-\frac{1}{n_{t,2}}\mathbf{W}_{t,2}^{\top}\mathbf{W}_{t,2}\eta^{*}=a-\hat{\Omega}_{n,W,t}\eta^{*}\,.

As a consequence,

\displaystyle\left\|a-\mathbf{W}_{t,2}^{\top}\tau_{1}^{0}\right\|_{\infty}

\displaystyle=\left\|a-a^{*}+a^{*}-a-\mathbf{W}_{t,2}^{\top}\tau_{1}^{0}\right\|_{\infty}

\displaystyle=\left\|a-a^{*}+(\Omega_{W,t}-\hat{\Omega}_{n,W,t})\eta^{*}\right\|_{\infty}

\displaystyle\leq\|a-a^{*}\|_{\infty}+\|\Omega_{W,t}-\hat{\Omega}_{n,W,t}\|_{\infty,\infty}\|\eta^{*}\|_{1}

\displaystyle\leq\|\bar{X}-\mu_{X}\|_{\infty}+\|\hat{m}_{c,1}-\mathbf{B}_{0}\mu_{X}\|_{\infty}+C_{\eta}\|\Omega_{W,t}-\hat{\Omega}_{n,W,t}\|_{\infty,\infty}

\displaystyle\leq(1+\|\mathbf{B}_{0}\|_{1,\infty})\|\bar{X}-\mu_{X}\|_{\infty}+\|\hat{m}_{c,1}-\mathbf{B}_{0}\bar{X}\|_{\infty}+C_{\eta}\|\Omega_{W,t}-\hat{\Omega}_{n,W,t}\|_{\infty,\infty}

\displaystyle\leq C\|\bar{X}-\mu_{X}\|_{\infty}+\|\mathbf{B}_{0}(\mathbf{X}_{c,1}^{\top}\hat{\tau}^{(1)}_{2}-\bar{X})\|_{\infty}+\|(\mathbf{U}_{c}^{(1)})^{\top}\hat{\tau}_{2}^{(1)}\|_{\infty}

\displaystyle\hskip 184.99878pt+C_{\eta}\|\Omega_{W,t}-\hat{\Omega}_{n,W,t}\|_{\infty,\infty}

\displaystyle\leq C\left(\|\bar{X}-\mu_{X}\|_{\infty}+\|(\mathbf{X}_{c,1}^{\top}\hat{\tau}^{(1)}_{2}-\bar{X})\|_{\infty}+\|\Omega_{W,t}-\hat{\Omega}_{n,W,t}\|_{\infty,\infty}\right)+\|(\mathbf{U}_{c}^{(1)})^{\top}\hat{\tau}_{2}^{(1)}\|_{\infty}

In the previous inequalities, we have used the fact that
\|\eta^{*}\|_{1}\leq C_{\eta}
(Assumption
5.3
) and
\|\mathbf{B}_{0}\|_{1,\infty}\leq C_{\mathbf{B}}
(Assumption
5.4
).
The first and second summands on the right-hand side are
\leq C\sqrt{\log{p}/n}
for a large enough constant
C
with probability going to
1
by standard sub-Gaussian concentration for
\bar{X}
and the definition of
\hat{\tau}_{2}^{(1)}
(Equation (
3.1
)). For the fourth term, we use the fact that conditionally on
\mathbf{X}_{c}
,
U_{c,i}
’s independent sub-Gaussian vector with sub-Gaussian constants uniformly bounded by
\sigma_{U}
. Therefore, conditionally on
\mathbf{X}
,

\textstyle\|(\mathbf{U}_{c}^{(1)})^{\top}\hat{\tau}_{2}^{(1)}\|_{\infty}=O_{p}\left(\|\hat{\tau}_{2}^{(1)}\|_{2}\ \sqrt{\log{q}}\right)=O_{p}\left(\sqrt{\frac{\log{q}}{n}}\right)

where the last conclusion follows from the fact that
n\|\hat{\tau}_{2}^{(1)}\|_{2}^{2}=O_{p}(1)
as established previously. Last but not least
\|\Omega_{W,t}-\hat{\Omega}_{n,W,t}\|_{\infty,\infty}=O_{p}(\sqrt{\log{(p+q)}/n})
which follows from standard sub-exponential Bernstein inequality, along with a union bound on the co-ordinates of
\hat{\Omega}_{n,W,t}
. Therefore, we conclude:

\textstyle\left\|a^{(1)}-\mathbf{W}_{t,2}^{\top}\tau_{1}^{0}\right\|_{\infty}\leq K_{1}\sqrt{\frac{\log{(p+q)}}{n_{t,2}}}

with probability going to
1
for large enough constant
K_{1}
. We next argue that
\|\tau_{1}^{0}\|_{\infty}\leq n_{t,2}^{-\alpha}
. Towards that end:

\displaystyle\max_{1\leq i\leq n_{t,2}}|\tau_{1,i}^{0}|

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{n_{t,2}}\ \max_{1\leq i\leq n_{t,2}}\ |W_{i}^{\top}\eta^{*}|

\displaystyle\leq\frac{|\mu_{W,1}^{\top}\eta^{*}|}{n_{t}}+\sqrt{\lambda_{\max}(\Sigma_{W,1})}\ \frac{1}{n_{t,2}}\ \max_{1\leq i\leq n_{t,2}}\ |\Sigma_{W,1}^{-1/2}(W_{i}-\mu_{W,1})^{\top}\eta^{*}|

\displaystyle\leq O_{p}\left(\frac{1}{n_{t,2}}\right)+CO_{p}\left(\frac{\sqrt{\log{n_{t,2}}}}{n_{t,2}}\right)\leq n_{t,2}^{-\alpha}

for any
\alpha\in(0,1)
. Here we have used the fact that
|\mu_{W,1}^{\top}\eta^{*}|\leq C
which follows from the fact that
\|\mu_{W,1}\|_{\infty}\leq C_{\mu}
,
\|\eta^{*}\|_{1}\leq C_{\eta}
,
\lambda_{\max}(\Sigma_{W,1})\leq C
and
\Sigma_{W,1}^{-1/2}(W_{i}-\mu_{W,1})
is sub-Gaussian (Assumption
5.3
and Remark
5.6
). Finally, we will show that
n\|\hat{\tau}_{1}^{(2)}\|_{2}^{2}=O_{p}(1)
by showing that
n\|\tau_{1}^{0}\|_{2}^{2}=O_{p}(1)
and then using the fact that
n\|\hat{\tau}_{1}^{(2)}\|_{2}^{2}\leq n\|\tau_{1}^{0}\|_{2}^{2}
as
\hat{\tau}_{1}^{(2)}
is the optimal solution of Equation (
3.2
), whereas
\tau_{1}^{0}
is only a feasible solution. Now for
\tau_{1}^{0}
, we have:

\textstyle n\|\tau_{1}^{0}\|_{2}^{2}=\frac{nn_{2}}{n_{t,2}^{2}}\ \frac{1}{n_{2}}\sum_{i\in\mathcal{D}_{2}}(W_{i}^{\top}\eta^{*})^{2}\mathds{1}_{A_{i}=1}\leq(\eta^{*})^{\top}\hat{\Omega}\hat{\eta}^{*}\leq\|\hat{\Omega}\|_{\infty}\|\eta^{*}\|_{1}^{2}\,.

Now
\|\hat{\Omega}\|_{\infty}=O_{p}(1)
as
\|\mathbb{E}[WW^{\top}\mid A=1]\|_{\infty}\leq\|\Sigma_{W,1}\|_{\infty}+\|\mu_{W,1}\|_{\infty}^{2}
. (Assumption
5.3
). Furthemore,
\|\eta^{*}\|_{1}\leq C_{\eta}
by Assumption
5.3
. Hence,
n\|\tau_{1}^{0}\|_{2}^{2}=O_{p}(1)
. This completes the proof.

∎

B.2
Proof of Theorem
5.9

Proof.

From Proposition
4.1
, we have:

\frac{\sqrt{n}(\hat{\theta}_{10}^{\rm cf}-\theta_{10})}{\sigma_{n}^{\rm cf}}=\frac{\sqrt{n}(\Delta_{n}^{\rm cf}+V_{n}^{\rm cf})}{\sigma_{n}^{\rm cf}}\,.

(B.2)

Therefore, we divide the rest of the proof into three parts:

a)

We show that
\sqrt{n}\Delta_{n}^{\rm cf}=o_{p}(1)
,

b)

We show that
\sigma_{n}^{{\it cf.}}
is bounded away from 0 with probability going to
1
.

c)

We show that
\sqrt{n}V_{n}^{\rm cf}/\sigma_{n}^{\rm cf}
converges to
\mathcal{N}(0,1)
.

Asymptotic negligibility of
\sqrt{n}\Delta_{n}^{\rm cf}
.
We show that
\sqrt{n}|\Delta_{n,1}|=o_{p}(1)
. The proof for
\Delta_{n,2}
is same and hence skipped. For our proof we will use upper on
|\Delta_{n,1}|
as established in Proposition
4.1
.
By standard analysis to establish the convergence rate of the Lasso estimator with respect to the
\ell_{1}
-norm under the sparsity constraint (e.g.,
(
Negahban et al., 2012
, Corollary 2)
), we have the following (we omit the notation
j
from the next paragraph for notational simplicity, as the argument holds for each
j\in\{1,2\}
):

\textstyle\|\hat{\phi}_{1}-\phi_{1}\|_{1}=\mathcal{O}_{p}\left(k\sqrt{\frac{\log(p+q)}{n_{t}}}\right),\ \ \ \|\hat{b}-b\|_{1}=\mathcal{O}_{p}\left(sk_{\gamma}\sqrt{\frac{\log(p)}{n_{c}}}+k\sqrt{\frac{\log{(p+q)}}{n_{t}}}\right)\,.

(B.3)

Note that in order to apply Corollary 2 of
Negahban et al. (2012)
, we need the design matrices to satisfy the RE condition, and the columns should be approximately standardized. Regarding the RE condition, we need
(\mathbf{W}_{t}^{\top}\mathbf{W}_{t})/n_{t}
to satisfy the RE condition with high probability for establishing the bound on
\|\hat{\phi}_{1}-\phi_{1}\|_{1}
and
(\mathbf{X}_{c}^{\top}\mathbf{X}_{c})/n_{c}
to satisfy the RE condition with high probability to establish the bound on
\|\hat{b}-b\|_{1}
. For the former, note that, by Assumption
5.3
, the population second moment matrix
\mathbb{E}[WW^{\top}\mid A=1]=\Sigma_{W,t}+\mu_{W,t}\mu_{W,t}^{\top}\succeq\Sigma_{W,t}
has minimum eigenvalue bounded away from
0
and
\mathbf{W}_{t}
has i.i.d. sub-Gaussian rows (Assumption
5.3
and Remark
5.6
). Therefore, by
(
Rudelson and Zhou, 2012
, Theorem 6)
, we can conclude that
(\mathbf{W}_{t}^{\top}\mathbf{W}_{t})/n_{t}
also satisfies RE condition with probability going to
1
. Similar logic also applies for
(\mathbf{X}_{c}^{\top}\mathbf{X}_{c})/n_{c}
and its population second moment matrix
\mathbb{E}[XX^{\top}\mid A=0]
as minimum eigenvalues bounded away from
0
and
\mathbf{X}_{c}
has i.i.d. sub-Gaussian rows (Assumption
5.3
). Now, as for column normalization, we need
\|\mathbf{W}_{t,*j}\|_{2}/\sqrt{n_{t}}\leq C
and
\|\mathbf{X}_{c,*j}\|_{2}/\sqrt{n_{c}}\leq C
for some constant
C>0
with probability going to
1
. This follows from standard sub-Gaussian concentration; For
\mathbf{W}_{t}
, fix
1\leq j\leq p+q
and set

\textstyle\mu_{W,t,j}^{(2)}=(\Sigma_{W,t}+\mu_{W,t}\mu_{W,t}^{\top})_{jj}=\mathbb{E}[W_{t,ij}^{2}]\,.\vskip-9.24994pt

Therefore, we have:

\displaystyle\max_{1\leq j\leq p+q}\frac{1}{n_{t}}\sum_{i\in\mathcal{D}_{2}:A_{i}=1}W_{t,ij}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq\max_{1\leq j\leq p+q}\left|\frac{1}{n_{t}}\sum_{i\in\mathcal{D}_{2}:A_{i}=1}(W_{t,ij}^{2}-\mu^{(2)}_{W,t,j})\right|+\max_{1\leq j\leq p+q}\mu^{(2)}_{W,t,j}

\displaystyle\leq\max_{1\leq j\leq p+q}\mu^{(2)}_{W,t,j}+C\sqrt{\frac{\log{(p+q)}}{n}},

(B.4)

with probability going to
1
. The last bound follows from a standard sub-Gaussian concentration argument. We next verify that
\max_{1\leq j\leq p+q}\mu_{W,t,j}^{(2)}
is uniformly bounded. Recall that
\mu_{W,t,j}^{(2)}=(\Sigma_{W,t})_{jj}+\mu_{W,t,j}^{2}
. Therefore, by Remark
5.6
and Assumptions
5.3
and
5.4
,
\lambda_{\max}(\Sigma_{W,t})\leq C_{W}:=(1+C_{B})^{2}(C_{X}\vee C_{U})
, where
C_{X}
,
C_{U}
, and
C_{B}
are the constants as defined in
Assumptions 2 and 3. Moreover, since
\mu_{W,t}=(\mu_{X,1}^{\top},(B_{1}\mu_{X,1})^{\top})^{\top}
, we have

\textstyle\|\mu_{W,t}\|_{\infty}\leq\max\left\{\|\mu_{X,1}\|_{\infty},\ \|B_{1}\|_{1,\infty}\|\mu_{X,1}\|_{\infty}\right\}\leq(1\vee C_{B})C_{\mu}=:C_{\mu,W}.

As a consequence,
\max_{1\leq j\leq p+q}\mu_{W,t,j}^{(2)}\leq C_{W}+C_{\mu,W}^{2}:=C_{W,2}<\infty
. Therefore, Equation (
B.4
) implies that, with probability going to one,

\textstyle\max_{1\leq j\leq p+q}\frac{1}{n_{t}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}i\in\mathcal{D}_{2}\\
A_{i}=1\end{subarray}}W_{t,ij}^{2}\leq 2C_{W,2}\implies\max_{1\leq j\leq p+q}\frac{\|\mathbf{W}_{t,*j}\|_{2}}{\sqrt{n_{t}}}\leq\sqrt{2C_{W,2}}\,,

with probability going to one. Similarly, for the control-arm covariate design,
\mathbb{E}[X_{j}^{2}\mid A=0]=(\Sigma_{X,0})_{jj}+\mu_{X,0,j}^{2}\leq C_{X}+C_{\mu}^{2}:=C_{X,2}
. The same concentration argument therefore gives
\max_{1\leq j\leq p}\|\mathbf{X}_{c,*j}\|_{2}/\sqrt{n_{c}}\leq\sqrt{2C_{X,2}}
with probability going to one.
Therefore, conditions required for Corollary 2.1 of
Negahban et al. (2012)
are satisfied by the design matrices
\mathbf{W}_{t}
and
\mathbf{X}_{c}
, and consequently Equation (
B.3
) holds.
The rate for
\|\hat{b}-b\|_{1}
needs some additional calculation as
b=\mathbf{B}_{0}^{\top}\hat{\gamma}_{1}
may not be exactly sparse and is deferred to Lemma
C.3
.
Now, going back to
\Delta_{n,1}
, we have, from (
4.2
):

\displaystyle\sqrt{n}|\Delta_{n,1}|

\displaystyle\leq\sqrt{n}\|\mathbf{B}_{0}(\bar{X}-\mathbf{X}_{c,j}^{\top}\hat{\tau}_{2}^{(1)})\|_{\infty}\|\hat{\gamma}^{(2)}_{1}-\gamma_{1}\|_{1}

\displaystyle\hskip 18.49988pt+\sqrt{n}\|a^{(1)}-\mathbf{W}_{t,2}^{\top}\hat{\tau}^{(2)}_{1}\|_{\infty}\|\hat{\phi}_{1}^{(2)}-\phi_{1}\|_{1}

\displaystyle\hskip 18.49988pt+\sqrt{n}\|\bar{X}-\mathbf{X}_{c,1}^{\top}\hat{\tau}^{(1)}_{2}\|_{\infty}\|\hat{b}^{(1)}-b^{(2)}\|_{1}:=T_{1}+T_{2}+T_{3}\,.

Now, by definition of
\hat{\tau}_{2}^{(1)}
(Step 1 of Algorithm
1
), and the fact that
\|\mathbf{B}_{0}\|_{1,\infty}\leq C_{\mathbf{B}}
(Assumption
5.4
), we have:

\displaystyle T_{1}

\displaystyle\leq\sqrt{n}\|\mathbf{B}_{0}\|_{1,\infty}\|\bar{X}-\mathbf{X}_{c,j}^{\top}\hat{\tau}_{2}^{(j)}\|_{\infty}\|\hat{\gamma}^{(2)}_{1}-\gamma_{1}\|_{1}

\displaystyle\leq C_{\mathbf{B}}K_{2}\sqrt{n}\sqrt{\frac{\log{p}}{n_{c,1}}}\|\hat{\gamma}^{(2)}_{1}-\gamma_{1}\|_{1}

\displaystyle=O_{p}\left(\sqrt{n}\times\sqrt{\frac{\log{p}}{n_{c,1}}}\times k\sqrt{\frac{\log(p+q)}{n_{t}}}\right)=o_{p}(1)

The last conclusion follows from Assumption
5.4
. Now, from Equation (
3.2
), we have
\|a^{(1)}-\mathbf{W}_{t,2}^{\top}\hat{\tau}_{1}^{(2)}\|_{\infty}\leq K_{1}\sqrt{\log{(p+q)}/n_{t,2}}
. Therefore, this, along with Equation (
B.3
) implies:

\displaystyle T_{2}

\displaystyle=\sqrt{n}\|a^{(1)}-\mathbf{W}_{t,2}^{\top}\hat{\tau}^{(2)}_{1}\|_{\infty}\|\hat{\phi}_{1}^{(2)}-\phi_{1}\|_{1}

\displaystyle=O_{p}\left(\sqrt{n}\times\sqrt{\frac{\log{(p+q)}}{n}}\times k\sqrt{\frac{\log{(p+q)}}{n}}\right)=O_{p}\left(\frac{k\log{(p+q)}}{\sqrt{n}}\right)=o_{p}(1),

where the last conclusion follows from Assumption
5.4
. Similarly for
T_{3}
, we have
\|\bar{X}-\mathbf{X}_{c,1}^{\top}\hat{\tau}^{(1)}_{2}\|_{\infty}\leq K_{2}\sqrt{\log{p}/n_{c,1}}
from Equation (
3.1
). Therefore, this and Equation (
B.3
) implies:

\displaystyle T_{3}

\displaystyle=\sqrt{n}\|\bar{X}-\mathbf{X}_{c}^{\top}\tau_{2}\|_{\infty}\|\hat{b}-b\|_{1}

\displaystyle=O_{p}\left(\sqrt{n}\times\sqrt{\frac{\log{(p)}}{n}}\times\left(sk_{\gamma}\sqrt{\frac{\log{p}}{n}}+k\sqrt{\frac{\log{(p+q)}}{n}}\right)\right)

\displaystyle=O_{p}\left(\frac{sk_{\gamma}\log{p}}{\sqrt{n}}+\frac{k\log{(p+q)}}{\sqrt{n}}\right)=o_{p}(1),

where, as before, the last conclusion follows from Assumption
5.4
. This completes the proof that
\sqrt{n}|\Delta_{n,1}|=o_{p}(1)
.

Lower bound on
\sigma_{n}^{\rm cf}
:
Our next goal is to show that
\sigma_{n}^{\rm cf}
remains bounded away from zero with probability tending to one. Recall that

\displaystyle(\sigma_{n}^{\rm cf})^{2}=\frac{n}{4}\sum_{j=1}^{2}(\sigma_{1}^{2}\lVert\hat{\tau}_{1}^{(j)}\rVert_{2}^{2}+\gamma_{1}^{\top}\Sigma_{U,0}\gamma_{1}\lVert\hat{\tau}_{2}^{(j)}\rVert_{2}^{2})\geq\frac{n\sigma_{1}^{2}\lVert\hat{\tau}_{1}^{(1)}\rVert_{2}^{2}}{4}\,.

(B.5)

Therefore, we next show that
n\|\hat{\tau}_{1}^{(1)}\|_{2}^{2}\geq c
for some constant
c>0
with probability going to
1
. This, along with the fact that
\sigma_{1}^{2}>0
(Assumption
5.3
) will yield the conclusion. In fact, we will establish both
n\|\hat{\tau}_{1}^{(2)}\|_{2}^{2}
and
n\|\hat{\tau}_{2}^{(1)}\|_{2}^{2}
are bounded away from
0
with probability going to
1
, which will be used in the later part of the proof. The conclusion for the balancing weights using another fold of the data (i.e.,
(\hat{\tau}_{1}^{(1)},\hat{\tau}_{2}^{(2)})
) is similar and hence skipped. We start with
n\|\hat{\tau}^{(2)}_{1}\|_{2}^{2}
. From the definition of
a^{(1)}
, we have:

\displaystyle\|a^{(1)}\|_{\infty}\geq\|\bar{X}\|_{\infty}\geq\|\mu_{X}\|_{\infty}-\|\bar{X}-\mu_{X}\|_{\infty}

\displaystyle\geq\kappa-\|\bar{X}-\mu_{X}\|_{\infty}\geq\kappa/2

where the last inequality follows with probability approaching to
1
as
\|\bar{X}-\mu_{X}\|_{\infty}=O_{p}(\sqrt{\log{p}/n})
from standard sub-Gaussian concentration and
\kappa>0
. Here we have also used the fact that
\|\mu_{X}\|_{\infty}\geq\kappa
(Assumption
5.3
). Now on this event, there exists
1\leq j\leq p+q
such that
|a_{j}|\geq\kappa/2
and
1\leq j\leq p
such that
|\bar{X}_{j}|\geq\kappa/2
.
For any feasible solution
\tau_{1}
of Equation (
3.2
) and
\tau_{2}
of Equation (
3.1
) satisfy for all large
n
:

\displaystyle|(\mathbf{W}_{t,2}^{\top}\tau_{1})_{j}|

\displaystyle\geq|a^{(1)}_{j}|-|a^{(1)}_{j}-(\mathbf{W}_{t,2}^{\top}\tau_{1})_{j}|\geq\kappa/2-K_{1}\sqrt{\frac{\log(p+q)}{n_{t}}}\geq\kappa/4,

\displaystyle|(\mathbf{X}_{c,1}^{\top}\tau_{2})_{j}|

\displaystyle\geq|\bar{X}_{j}|-|\bar{X}_{j}-(\mathbf{X}_{c,1}^{\top}\tau_{2})_{j}|\geq\kappa/2-K_{2}\sqrt{\frac{\log p}{n_{c}}}\geq\kappa/4,

with probability tending to one, since
\log(p+q)/(n_{t}\wedge n_{c})\to 0
. Applying Cauchy–Schwarz yields

\displaystyle\kappa_{1}^{2}\leq(\mathbf{W}_{t,2}^{\top}\tau_{1})_{j}^{2}\leq\|\tau_{1}\|_{2}^{2}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}i\in\mathcal{D}_{2}\\
A_{i}=1\end{subarray}}W_{t,ij}^{2},\hskip 18.49988pt\kappa_{2}^{2}\leq(\mathbf{X}_{c}^{\top}\tau_{2})_{j}^{2}\leq\|\tau_{2}\|_{2}^{2}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}i\in\mathcal{D}_{1}\\
A_{i}=0\end{subarray}}X_{c,ij}^{2}.

Therefore,

\displaystyle n\|\tau_{1}\|_{2}^{2}\geq\frac{\kappa^{2}/16}{\frac{n_{t,2}}{n}\cdot\frac{1}{n_{t}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}i\in\mathcal{D}_{2}\\
A_{i}=1\end{subarray}}W_{t,ij}^{2}},\hskip 18.49988ptn\|\tau_{2}\|_{2}^{2}\geq\frac{\kappa^{2}/16}{\frac{n_{c,1}}{n}\cdot\frac{1}{n_{c}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}i\in\mathcal{D}_{1}\\
A_{i}=0\end{subarray}}X_{c,ij}^{2}}.

For the denominator,
n_{t,2}/n\leq 1
and
(\sum_{i:A_{i}=1}W^{2}_{t,ij})/n_{t,2}\leq 2\mu^{(2)}_{W,t,j}
, which follows from the same calculation as in Equation (
B.4
). Therefore, we conclude that with probability going to
1
,

n\|\hat{\tau}^{(2)}_{1}\|_{2}^{2}\ \wedge\ n\|\hat{\tau}^{(1)}_{2}\|_{2}^{2}\geq\frac{\kappa^{2}}{C}\ \ \text{with probability going to }1.

(B.6)

This completes the proof.

Asymptotic normality:
Finally we will establish the asymptotic normality of
\sqrt{n}V_{n}^{\rm cf}/\sigma_{n}^{\rm cf}
. Recall that, from Proposition
4.1
, we have:

\frac{\sqrt{n}V_{n}^{\rm cf}}{\sigma_{n}^{\rm cf}}=\frac{\sqrt{n}}{2\sigma_{n}^{\rm cf}}\left[\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}i\in\mathcal{D}_{2}\\
A_{i}=1\end{subarray}}\hat{\tau}^{(2)}_{1,i}{\epsilon}_{i}+\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}i\in\mathcal{D}_{1}\\
A_{i}=0\end{subarray}}\hat{\tau}^{(1)}_{2,i}U_{i}^{\top}\gamma_{1}+\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}i\in\mathcal{D}_{1}\\
A_{i}=1\end{subarray}}\hat{\tau}^{(1)}_{1,i}{\epsilon}_{i}+\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}i\in\mathcal{D}_{2}\\
A_{i}=0\end{subarray}}\hat{\tau}^{(2)}_{2,i}U_{i}^{\top}\gamma_{1}\right]:=T

where we introduce the notation
T
for notational simplicity. For the rest of the analysis define
\tau_{1}^{(j),0}
as
an oracle version of

\hat{\tau}^{(j)}_{1}
where
\tau_{1}^{(j),0}
is obtained by solving optimization problem Equation (
3.2
) with replacing
a^{(j^{\prime})}
by
a^{(j^{\prime}),0}\equiv a^{0}
where
a^{0}=(\bar{X}^{\top}\ \ (\mathbf{B}_{0}\bar{X})^{\top})^{\top}
. In other words, we obtain
a^{0}
by replacing the
\hat{m}_{c,j}
part by its oracle counterpart
\mathbf{B}_{0}\bar{X}
. Note that now
\{\hat{\tau}_{1}^{(j),0}\}
for
j\in\{1,2\}
only depends on
\mathcal{H}_{n}:=\sigma(A_{1:n},X_{1:n},\{M_{i}:A_{i}=1\})
. Furthermore, by definition,
\{\hat{\tau}_{2}^{(j)}\}
also is measurable function of
\sigma(A_{1:n},X_{1:n})
, and consequently a measurable function of
\mathcal{H}_{n}
. Our following Lemma shows that
\hat{\tau}_{1}^{(j),0}
is asymptotically close to
\hat{\tau}_{1}^{(j)}
:

Lemma B.1
.

Under Assumption
5.3
and
5.4
, we have:

\textstyle\sqrt{n}\|\hat{\tau}_{1}^{(j)}-\hat{\tau}_{1}^{(j),0}\|_{2}=o_{p}(1),\hskip 9.24994ptj\in\{1,2\}.

Now, using this orcale
\hat{\tau}_{1}^{(j),0}
, we define:

\displaystyle T^{0}

\displaystyle=\frac{\sqrt{n}}{2\sigma_{n}^{\rm cf,0}}\left[\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}i\in\mathcal{D}_{2}\\
A_{i}=1\end{subarray}}\hat{\tau}^{(2),0}_{1,i}{\epsilon}_{i}+\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}i\in\mathcal{D}_{1}\\
A_{i}=0\end{subarray}}\hat{\tau}^{(1)}_{2,i}U_{i}^{\top}\gamma_{1}+\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}i\in\mathcal{D}_{1}\\
A_{i}=1\end{subarray}}\hat{\tau}^{(1),0}_{1,i}{\epsilon}_{i}+\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}i\in\mathcal{D}_{2}\\
A_{i}=0\end{subarray}}\hat{\tau}^{(2)}_{2,i}U_{i}^{\top}\gamma_{1}\right]

\displaystyle:=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\zeta_{n,i}

where

\displaystyle\zeta_{n,i}

\displaystyle=\frac{\sqrt{n}}{2\sigma_{n}^{\rm cf,0}}\left\{{\epsilon}_{i}\left(\hat{\tau}^{(1),0}_{1,i}\mathds{1}_{i\in\mathcal{D}_{1},A_{i}=1}+\hat{\tau}^{(2),0}_{1,i}\mathds{1}_{i\in\mathcal{D}_{2},A_{i}=1}\right)\right.

\displaystyle\hskip 18.49988pt\hskip 18.49988pt\left.+U_{i}^{\top}\gamma_{1}\left(\hat{\tau}^{(1)}_{2,i}\mathds{1}_{i\in\mathcal{D}_{1},A_{i}=0}+\hat{\tau}^{(2)}_{2,i}\mathds{1}_{i\in\mathcal{D}_{2},A_{i}=0}\right)\right\}

\displaystyle(\sigma_{n}^{\rm cf,0})^{2}

\displaystyle=\frac{n}{4}\sum_{j=1}^{2}(\sigma_{1}^{2}\lVert\hat{\tau}_{1}^{(j),0}\rVert_{2}^{2}+\gamma_{1}^{\top}\Sigma_{U,0}\gamma_{1}\lVert\hat{\tau}_{2}^{(j)}\rVert_{2}^{2})

(B.7)

We next show that
T^{0}
is asymptotically normal and then we will show that
T-T^{0}=o_{p}(1)
. This would complete the proof. To establish that
T^{0}
is asymptotically normal, first observe that conditional on
\mathcal{H}_{n}
the weights
\{(\hat{\tau}_{1}^{(j),0},\hat{\tau}_{2}^{(j)}):j\in\{1,2\}\}
are fixed and
(U_{i},{\epsilon}_{i})
are independent across
i
. From
\mathbb{E}[{\epsilon}_{i}\mid X_{i},M_{i},A_{i}=1]=0
and
\mathbb{E}[U_{i}\mid X_{i},A_{i}=0]=0
(Assumption
5.3
), we also have
\mathbb{E}[\zeta_{n,i}\mid\mathcal{H}_{n}]=0
and

\displaystyle{\sf var}(T^{0}\mid\mathcal{H}_{n})

\displaystyle=\sum_{i}{\sf var}(\zeta_{n,i}\mid\mathcal{H}_{n})

\displaystyle=\frac{n}{4(\sigma_{n}^{\rm cf,0})^{2}}\sum_{j=1}^{2}(\sigma_{1}^{2}\lVert\hat{\tau}_{1}^{(j),0}\rVert_{2}^{2}+\gamma_{1}^{\top}\Sigma_{U,0}\gamma_{1}\lVert\hat{\tau}_{2}^{(j)}\rVert_{2}^{2})=1\,.

Here we have used the fact that
{\sf var}({\epsilon}_{i}\mid X_{i},M_{i},A_{i}=1)=\sigma_{1}^{2}
and
{\sf var}(U_{i}\mid X_{i},A_{i}=0)=\Sigma_{U,0}
(Assumption
5.3
).
Now, we will verify Lyapunov’s condition for the third moment, i.e., we show that
\sum_{i}\mathbb{E}[|\zeta_{n,i}|^{3}\mid\mathcal{H}_{n}]\to 0
in probability. Towards that end, we will use the fact that
{\epsilon}_{i}
and
U_{i}^{\top}\gamma_{1}/\|\gamma_{1}\|_{2}
are sub-Gaussian (uniformly over
(X,M,A)
) and consequently they have finite third moment (Assumption
5.3
). For notational simplicity, we will use
C
to denote these bounds, i.e.
\mathbb{E}[|{\epsilon}_{i}|^{3}\mid\mathcal{H}_{n}]\leq C
and
\mathbb{E}[|U_{i}^{\top}\gamma_{1}|^{3}\mid\mathcal{H}_{n}]\leq C\|\gamma_{1}\|_{2}^{3}
. Using this we have:

\displaystyle\sum_{i}\mathbb{E}[|\zeta_{n,i}|^{3}\mid\mathcal{H}_{n}]

\displaystyle\leq C\frac{n^{3/2}}{8(\sigma_{n}^{\rm cf,0})^{3}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\left[|\hat{\tau}_{1,i}^{(1),0}|^{3}\mathds{1}_{i\in D_{1},A=1}+|\hat{\tau}_{1,i}^{(2),0}|^{3}\mathds{1}_{i\in D_{2},Ai=1}\right.

\displaystyle\hskip 18.49988pt\hskip 18.49988pt\hskip 18.49988pt\hskip 18.49988pt\hskip 18.49988pt\hskip 18.49988pt\left.+|\hat{\tau}_{2,i}^{(1)}|^{3}\mathds{1}_{i\in\mathcal{D}_{1},A_{i}=0}+|\hat{\tau}_{2,i}^{(2)}|^{3}\mathds{1}_{i\in\mathcal{D}_{2},A_{i}=0}\right]

\displaystyle\leq C\frac{n^{3/2}}{8(\sigma_{n}^{\rm cf,0})^{3}}\ \sum_{j=1}^{2}\left(\|\hat{\tau}_{1,i}^{(j),0}\|_{2}^{2}\|\hat{\tau}_{1,i}^{(j),0}\|_{\infty}+\|\hat{\tau}_{2}^{(j)}\|_{2}^{2}\|\hat{\tau}_{2,i}^{(j)}\|_{\infty}\right)\,.

In Lemma
5.8
we have established
n\|\hat{\tau}_{1,i}^{(j)}\|_{2}^{2}=O_{p}(1)
and
n\|\hat{\tau}_{2,i}^{(j)}\|_{2}^{2}=O_{p}(1)
. This, along with Lemma
B.1
, implies
n\|\hat{\tau}_{1}^{(j),0}\|_{2}^{2}=O_{p}(1)
.
On the other hand, by definition of the optimization problem (Equation (
3.1
) and (
3.2
)), we have
\|\hat{\tau}_{1}^{(j),0}\|_{\infty}\vee\|\hat{\tau}_{2}^{(j)}\|\leq Cn^{-\alpha}
where we have used the fact that
n_{c,j}\sim n
and
n_{t,j}\sim n
with probability going to
1
as
\mathbb{P}(A=1)
is bounded away from
0
and
1
, as well as each split has
n/2
observations.
(Recall that
\hat{\tau}_{1}^{(j),0}
is obtained by solving Equation (
3.2
) with
a^{(j^{\prime})}
replaced by
a^{0}
, so by feasibility it satisfies
\|\hat{\tau}_{1}^{(j),0}\|_{\infty}\leq n_{t,2}^{-\alpha}\leq Cn^{-\alpha}
). Therefore, we can conclude:

\textstyle n^{3/2}\sum_{j=1}^{2}\left(\|\hat{\tau}_{1,i}^{(j),0}\|_{2}^{2}\|\hat{\tau}_{1,i}^{(j),0}\|_{\infty}+\|\hat{\tau}_{2}^{(j)}\|_{2}^{2}\|\hat{\tau}_{2,i}^{(j)}\|_{\infty}\right)=O_{p}(n^{-1-\alpha+3/2})=o_{p}(1)

where the last conclusion follows from the fact that
\alpha>1/2
.
On the other hand, we have established in the previous part of the proof that
\sigma_{n}^{\rm cf}
is bounded away from
0
with probability going to
1
. We now argue that
\sigma_{n}^{\rm cf,0}
is also bounded away from 0 with high probability.

\displaystyle\left|(\sigma_{n}^{\rm cf})^{2}-(\sigma_{n}^{\rm cf,0})^{2}\right|

\displaystyle=\frac{n\sigma_{1}^{2}}{4}\sum_{j=1}^{2}|\lVert\hat{\tau}_{1}^{(j),0}\rVert_{2}^{2}-\lVert\hat{\tau}_{1}^{(j)}\rVert_{2}^{2}|

\displaystyle\leq\frac{n\sigma_{1}^{2}}{4}\sum_{j=1}^{2}\|\hat{\tau}_{1}^{(j),0}+\hat{\tau}_{1}^{(j)}\|_{2}\|\hat{\tau}_{1}^{(j),0}-\hat{\tau}_{1}^{(j)}\|_{2}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{\sigma_{1}^{2}}{4}\sum_{j=1}^{2}\left(\sqrt{n}(\|\hat{\tau}_{1}^{(j),0}\|_{2}+\|\hat{\tau}_{1}^{(j)}\|_{2})\right)\left(\sqrt{n}\|\hat{\tau}_{1}^{(j),0}-\hat{\tau}_{1}^{(j)}\|_{2}\right)

\displaystyle=o_{p}(1)\,.

(B.8)

Here, the last conclusion follows from the fact that
\sqrt{n}\|\hat{\tau}_{1}^{(j),0}-\hat{\tau}_{1}^{(j)}\|_{2}=o_{p}(1)
(Lemma
B.1
) and
\sqrt{n}(\|\hat{\tau}_{1}^{(j),0}\|_{2}+\|\hat{\tau}_{1}^{(j)}\|_{2})=O_{p}(1)
(Lemma
5.8
and Lemma
B.1
). As a consequence,
\sigma_{n}^{\rm cf,0}
will be bounded away from
0
with probability going to
1
. Hence,
\sum_{i}\mathbb{E}[|\zeta_{n,i}|^{3}\mid\mathcal{H}_{n}]=o_{p}(1)
. Therefore, by an application of conditional central limit theorem
Bulinski (2017)
, we conclude
T^{0}\xrightarrow{d}\mathcal{N}(0,1)
.

Next we argue that
T-T^{0}=o_{p}(1)
which would establish that
T\xrightarrow{d}\mathcal{N}(0,1)
. Towards that end, write
T=L/\sigma_{n}^{\rm cf}
and
T^{0}=L^{0}/\sigma_{n}^{\rm cf,0}
. Therefore, we can split the difference
T-T^{0}
into two parts:

T-T^{0}=\frac{L}{\sigma_{n}^{\rm cf}}-\frac{L^{0}}{\sigma_{n}^{\rm cf,0}}=\frac{L-L^{0}}{\sigma_{n}^{\rm cf}}+\frac{L^{0}}{\sigma_{n}^{\rm cf,0}}\left(\frac{\sigma_{n}^{\rm cf,0}}{\sigma_{n}^{\rm cf}}-1\right):=T_{1}+T_{2}\,.

(B.9)

Our goal is to shwo that both
T_{1}
and
T_{2}
are
o_{p}(1)
. For
T_{1}
, it is enough to show that
L-L^{0}=o_{p}(1)
as we have already established that
\sigma_{n}^{\rm cf}
is bounded away from
0
. Towards that end, define
\mathcal{G}_{n}=\sigma(A_{1:n},X_{1:n},M_{1:n})
. We have:

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[(L-L^{0})^{2}\mid\mathcal{G}_{n}]

\displaystyle=\frac{n}{4}\mathbb{E}\left[\left(\sum_{i\in\mathcal{D}_{2},A_{i}=1}(\hat{\tau}^{(2)}_{1,i}-\hat{\tau}^{(2),0}_{1,i}){\epsilon}_{i}+\sum_{i\in\mathcal{D}_{1},A_{i}=1}(\hat{\tau}^{(1)}_{1,i}-\hat{\tau}^{(1),0}_{1,i}){\epsilon}_{i}\right)^{2}\mid\mathcal{G}_{n}\right]

\displaystyle=\frac{n\sigma_{1}^{2}}{4}\sum_{j=1}^{2}\|\hat{\tau}^{(j)}_{1}-\hat{\tau}^{(j),0}_{1}\|_{2}^{2}=o_{p}(1)\,.

where the last conclusion again follows from Lemma
B.1
.
Therefore
|L-L^{0}|=o_{p}(1)
and consequently
T_{1}=o_{p}(1)
. For
T_{2}
, we have already shown
|(\sigma_{n}^{\rm cf})^{2}-(\sigma_{n}^{\rm cf,0})^{2}|=o_{p}(1)
. This, along with the fact that
\sigma_{n}^{\rm cf}
and
\sigma_{n}^{\rm cf.0}
are bounded away from
0
, implies
|\sigma_{n}^{\rm cf}-\sigma_{n}^{\rm cf,0}|=o_{p}(1)
. Now, this, along with the fact that
\sigma_{n}^{\rm cf}
is bounded away from
0
immplies
(\sigma_{n}^{\rm cf.0}/\sigma_{n}^{\rm cf}-1)=o_{p}(1)
. As we have already established
L^{0}/\sigma_{n}^{\rm cf.0}
is asymptotically normal (hence
O_{p}(1)
), we therefore have
T_{2}=o_{p}(1)
. This completes the proof that
T\xrightarrow{d}\mathcal{N}(0,1)
and hence concludes Theorem
5.9
.
∎

B.3
Proof of Corollary
5.11

Proof.

First, we claim, as a corollary of Theorem
5.9
that our proof essentially yields the following conditional version of the central limit theorem:

Lemma B.2

(Conditional asymptotic normality)
.

Let
\mathcal{X}_{n}=\sigma(X_{1:n})
be the
\sigma
-field generated by
\{X_{1},\ldots,X_{n}\}
. Under the same assumptions as Theorem
5.9
, we have, for every
t\in{\mathbb{R}}
,

\mathbb{P}\left(\frac{\sqrt{n}(\hat{\theta}^{\rm cf}_{10}-\theta_{10})}{\sigma_{n}^{\rm cf}}\leq t\,\middle|\,\mathcal{X}_{n}\right)\longrightarrow\Phi(t),\hskip 18.49988pt\text{in probability}.

Here
\Phi(t)
denotes the CDF of the standard normal distribution.

The proof of this Lemma is deferred to Section
C
.
Now, we present the proof of Corollary
5.11
using this lemma. Towards that end, let us define
A_{n}:=\sqrt{n}(\hat{\theta}_{10}^{\rm cf}-\theta_{10})
and
B_{n}:=\sqrt{n}(\theta_{10}-\theta_{10}^{\rm pop})
. Recall that, by definition,
\theta_{10}=\bar{X}^{\top}\Lambda
,
\theta_{10}^{\rm pop}=\mu_{X}^{\top}\Lambda
with
\Lambda=\beta_{1}+\mathbf{B}_{0}^{\top}\gamma_{1}
.
Therefore,

\textstyle B_{n}=\sqrt{n}(\bar{X}-\mu_{X})^{\top}\Lambda=\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}(X_{i}-\mu_{X})^{\top}\Lambda,

and consequently
B_{n}
is measurable with respect to
\mathcal{X}_{n}
. By Lemma
B.2
, for every fixed
u\in{\mathbb{R}}
,

\textstyle\mathbb{E}\left[{\sf exp}\left\{iu(A_{n}/\sigma_{n}^{\rm cf})\right\}\,\middle|\,\mathcal{X}_{n}\right]\xrightarrow{p}e^{-u^{2}/2}.

Since the absolute difference between the two characteristic functions is bounded by
2
, convergence in probability implies
convergence in
L_{1}
. Consequently,

\textstyle\mathbb{E}\left|\mathbb{E}\left[{\sf exp}\left\{iu(A_{n}/\sigma_{n}^{\rm cf})\right\}\,\middle|\,\mathcal{X}_{n}\right]-e^{-u^{2}/2}\right|\longrightarrow 0.

(B.10)

We next establish the asymptotic joint behavior of

(A_{n}/\sigma_{n}^{\rm cf},B_{n})
. For any fixed

u,v\in{\mathbb{R}}
, since
B_{n}
is measurable with respect to
\mathcal{X}_{n}
,

\textstyle\mathbb{E}\left[{\sf exp}\left\{iu(A_{n}/\sigma_{n}^{\rm cf})+ivB_{n}\right\}\right]=\mathbb{E}\left[e^{ivB_{n}}\mathbb{E}\left[{\sf exp}\left\{iu(A_{n}/\sigma_{n}^{\rm cf})\right\}\,\middle|\,\mathcal{X}_{n}\right]\right].

As a consequence,

\displaystyle\left|\mathbb{E}\left[{\sf exp}\left\{iu(A_{n}/\sigma_{n}^{\rm cf})+ivB_{n}\right\}\right]-e^{-u^{2}/2}\mathbb{E}[e^{ivB_{n}}]\right|

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{E}\left|\mathbb{E}\left[{\sf exp}\left\{iu(A_{n}/\sigma_{n}^{\rm cf})\right\}\,\middle|\,\mathcal{X}_{n}\right]-e^{-u^{2}/2}\right|\longrightarrow 0,

(B.11)

where the last conclusion follows from
Equation (
B.10
).
For the rest of the analysis define
\omega_{n}=(\sigma_{n}^{\rm cf})^{2}/(\sigma^{2}_{X}+(\sigma_{n}^{\rm cf})^{2})
. By our assumption, there exists a bounded deterministic sequence
\bar{\omega}_{n}
such that
\omega_{n}-\bar{\omega}_{n}\to 0
in probability.
As
\sigma_{X}^{2}\leq C
, given any sequence, there exists a further subsequence such that on that subsequence (we use the same notation
n
for the sub-sequence for notational simplicity)

\textstyle\sigma_{X}^{2}\longrightarrow\zeta^{2},\hskip 18.49988pt\bar{\omega}_{n}\longrightarrow\omega,

for some
\zeta^{2}\in[0,C]
and
\omega\in[0,1]
and consequently
\omega_{n}\xrightarrow{p}\omega
. By the sub-Gaussian design condition, the triangular array
\left\{(X_{i}-\mu_{X})^{\top}\Lambda:1\leq i\leq n\right\}
satisfies the Lindeberg condition, and consequently, along this sub-sequence, by Lindeberg-Feller CLT, we have:

\textstyle B_{n}\xrightarrow{d}Z_{2}\sim\mathcal{N}(0,\zeta^{2})\,.

Combining this conclusion with Equation (
B.11
), we have, for any fixed
u,v\in{\mathbb{R}}
,

\textstyle\mathbb{E}\left[{\sf exp}\left\{iu(A_{n}/\sigma_{n}^{\rm cf})+ivB_{n}\right\}\right]\longrightarrow e^{-u^{2}/2}e^{-v^{2}\zeta^{2}/2}.

This immediately implies,

\left(\frac{A_{n}}{\sigma_{n}^{\rm cf}},B_{n}\right)\xrightarrow{d}(Z_{1},Z_{2}),

(B.12)

where
Z_{1}\sim\mathcal{N}(0,1),Z_{2}\sim\mathcal{N}(0,\zeta^{2})
and
Z_{1}\perp\!\!\!\!\perp Z_{2}
.
Now define
D_{n}^{2}:=(\sigma_{n}^{\rm cf})^{2}+\sigma_{X}^{2}
. Then, from our definition of
\omega_{n}
, we have
\omega_{n}=(\sigma_{n}^{\rm cf})^{2}/D_{n}^{2}
, and consequently

\textstyle\textstyle(A_{n}+B_{n})/D_{n}=\sqrt{\omega_{n}}\ (A_{n}/\sigma_{n}^{\rm cf})+(B_{n}/D_{n}).

(B.13)

Furthermore, we also have
D_{n}=\sigma_{X}/\sqrt{1-\omega_{n}}
. First consider the case
\zeta>0
. Then
\omega_{n}\to\omega
in probability and
\sigma_{X}\to\zeta>0
. Therefore, by Slutsky’s theorem, along with Equation (
B.12
) we have:

\displaystyle\frac{A_{n}+B_{n}}{D_{n}}

\displaystyle\xrightarrow{d}\sqrt{\omega}\,Z_{1}+\frac{\sqrt{1-\omega}}{\zeta}Z_{2}\sim\mathcal{N}(0,1)\,.

Suppose next that
\zeta^{2}=0
. In this case,
\mathbb{E}[B_{n}^{2}]=\sigma_{X}^{2}\to 0
, and consequently
B_{n}=o_{p}(1)
. Furthermore, Theorem
5.9
establishes that, for
some constant
c_{0}>0
,
\mathbb{P}\left((\sigma_{n}^{\rm cf})^{2}\geq c_{0}\right)\to 1
and consequently
D_{n}
is also bounded away from 0 with probability going to
1
. Therefore,
\omega_{n}\to 1
in probability and
B_{n}/D_{n}=o_{p}(1)
.
Then it follows from Equation (
B.13
) that

\textstyle\frac{A_{n}+B_{n}}{D_{n}}=\frac{A_{n}}{\sigma_{n}^{\rm cf}}+o_{p}(1)\xrightarrow{d}\mathcal{N}(0,1),

where the last conclusion follows from Theorem
5.9
. Hence, we have proved that given any sequence, there exists a further subsequence along which

\textstyle\frac{A_{n}+B_{n}}{\sqrt{(\sigma_{n}^{\rm cf})^{2}+\sigma_{X}^{2}}}\xrightarrow{d}\mathcal{N}(0,1).

As the limiting distribution is the same for all converging subsequences, we conclude:

\textstyle\frac{A_{n}+B_{n}}{\sqrt{(\sigma_{n}^{\rm cf})^{2}+\sigma_{X}^{2}}}\xrightarrow{d}\mathcal{N}(0,1).

This completes the proof recalling
A_{n}+B_{n}=\sqrt{n}(\hat{\theta}_{10}^{\rm cf}-\theta_{10}^{\rm pop})
.
∎

B.4
Proof of Corollary
5.12

Proof.

For the first part, the proof is very closely related to the proof of Theorem
5.9
, so we will highlight the key differences here. The first part of the proof of Theorem
5.9
, i.e.,
\sqrt{n}\Delta_{n}^{\rm cf}=o_{p}(1)
, remains identical, as the lasso rates and the conclusion of Lemma
5.8
do not depend directly on the conditional heteroskedasticity as long as errors are uniformly sub-Gaussian. The second part, i.e., lower bounding
(\sigma_{n}^{\rm cf,het})^{2}
also remains almost identical upon observing that
{\sf var}({\epsilon}\mid X,M,A=1)\geq c_{-}
. The only difference comes in the third part. Recall the oracle balancing weights
\tau_{1}^{(j),0}
as introduced in the proof of Theorem
5.9
, which is obtained by replacing
a
with
a^{0}=(\bar{X}^{\top}\ \ (\mathbf{B}_{0}\bar{X})^{\top})^{\top}
in Step 3 of Algorithm
1
. Also recall the definition of
T
and
T^{0}
introduced in the proof of the asymptotic normality part of Theorem
5.9
with
\sigma_{n}^{\rm cf}
replaced by
\sigma_{n}^{\rm cf,het}
as defined in the statement of Corollary
5.12
. As before, we first establish that
T^{0}
converges to
\mathcal{N}(0,1)
, where recall that
T^{0}=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\zeta_{n,i,\rm het}
with:

\displaystyle\zeta_{n,i,\rm het}

\displaystyle=\frac{\sqrt{n}}{2\sigma_{n}^{\rm cf,het,0}}\left\{{\epsilon}_{i}\left(\hat{\tau}^{(1),0}_{1,i}\mathds{1}_{i\in\mathcal{D}_{1},A_{i}=1}+\hat{\tau}^{(2),0}_{1,i}\mathds{1}_{i\in\mathcal{D}_{2},A_{i}=1}\right)\right.

\displaystyle\hskip 18.49988pt\hskip 18.49988pt\hskip 18.49988pt\hskip 18.49988pt\hskip 18.49988pt\left.+U_{i}^{\top}\gamma_{1}\left(\hat{\tau}^{(1)}_{2,i}\mathds{1}_{i\in\mathcal{D}_{1},A_{i}=0}+\hat{\tau}^{(2)}_{2,i}\mathds{1}_{i\in\mathcal{D}_{2},A_{i}=0}\right)\right\}

\displaystyle(\sigma_{n}^{\rm cf,het,0})^{2}

\displaystyle=\frac{n}{4}\sum_{j=1}^{2}\left(\sum_{i\in\mathcal{D}_{j},A_{i}=1}\{(\hat{\tau}_{1,i}^{(j),0})^{2}\nu_{1}(X_{i},M_{i})\}+\gamma_{1}^{\top}\Sigma_{U,0}\gamma_{1}\lVert\hat{\tau}_{2}^{(j)}\rVert_{2}^{2}\right)\,.

Define
\mathcal{H}_{n}=\sigma(A_{1:n},X_{1:n},\{M_{i}:A_{i}=1\})
.
It is immediate that conditional on
\mathcal{H}_{n}
,
\{\zeta_{n,i,\rm het}\}
are independent across
i
.
Same calculation as in the proof of Theorem
5.9
yields
\mathbb{E}[\zeta_{n,i,\rm het}\mid\mathcal{H}_{n}]=0
and
{\sf var}(\sum_{i}\zeta_{n,i,\rm het}\mid\mathcal{H}_{n})=1
.
We next show that the difference between
(\sigma_{n}^{\rm cf,het})^{2}
and
(\sigma_{n}^{\rm cf,het,0})^{2}
is asymptotically negligible (similar to Equation (
B.8
)):

\displaystyle\left|(\sigma_{n}^{\rm cf,het})^{2}-(\sigma_{n}^{\rm cf,het,0})^{2}\right|

\displaystyle=\frac{n}{4}\sum_{j=1}^{2}\left[\sum_{i\in\mathcal{D}_{j},A_{i}=1}\left\{\left((\hat{\tau}_{1,i}^{(j)})^{2}-(\hat{\tau}_{1,i}^{(j),0})^{2}\right)\nu_{1}(X_{i},M_{i})\right\}\right]

\displaystyle\leq\frac{nc_{+}}{4}\sum_{j=1}^{2}\sum_{i\in\mathcal{D}_{j},A_{i}=1}\left|(\hat{\tau}_{1,i}^{(j)})^{2}-(\hat{\tau}_{1,i}^{(j),0})^{2}\right|

\displaystyle\leq\frac{nc_{+}}{4}\sum_{j=1}^{2}\sum_{i\in\mathcal{D}_{j},A_{i}=1}\left|\hat{\tau}_{1,i}^{(j)}+\hat{\tau}_{1,i}^{(j),0}\right|\left|\hat{\tau}_{1,i}^{(j)}-\hat{\tau}_{1,i}^{(j),0}\right|

\displaystyle\leq\frac{nc_{+}}{4}\sum_{j=1}^{2}\sqrt{\sum_{i\in\mathcal{D}_{j},A_{i}=1}\left|\hat{\tau}_{1,i}^{(j)}+\hat{\tau}_{1,i}^{(j),0}\right|^{2}}\sqrt{\sum_{i\in\mathcal{D}_{j},A_{i}=1}\left|\hat{\tau}_{1,i}^{(j)}-\hat{\tau}_{1,i}^{(j),0}\right|^{2}}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{nc_{+}}{2\sqrt{2}}\sum_{j=1}^{2}(\|\hat{\tau}_{1}^{(j)}\|_{2}+\|\hat{\tau}_{1}^{(j),0}\|_{2})\|\hat{\tau}_{1}^{(j)}-\hat{\tau}_{1}^{(j),0}\|_{2}

\displaystyle=\frac{c_{+}}{2\sqrt{2}}\sum_{j=1}^{2}(\sqrt{n}\|\hat{\tau}_{1}^{(j)}\|_{2}+\sqrt{n}\|\hat{\tau}_{1}^{(j),0}\|_{2})\sqrt{n}\|\hat{\tau}_{1}^{(j)}-\hat{\tau}_{1}^{(j),0}\|_{2}=o_{p}(1)\,.

(B.14)

The last conclusion follows from Lemma
5.8
and Lemma
B.1
. Therefore,

\sigma_{n}^{\rm cf,het,0}
is also bounded away from 0 as probability tend to
1
.
As a consequence,
\sum_{i}\mathbb{E}[|\zeta_{n,i,\rm het}|^{3}\mid\mathcal{H}_{n}]\to 0
by the exact same calculation as in the proof of Theorem
5.9
; the only difference is
(\sigma_{n}^{\rm cf,0})^{3}
is replaced by
(\sigma_{n}^{\rm cf,het,0})^{3}
but as we have argued,
\sigma_{n}^{\rm cf,het,0}
is bounded away from
0
with probability going to
1
as
\nu_{1}(x,m)\geq c_{-}>0
. Therefore, by conditional CLT,
T^{0}\xrightarrow{d}\mathcal{N}(0,1)
. Next, we need to show
T-T^{0}=o_{p}(1)
. Following same expansion as in Equation (
B.9
) we need to show
L-L^{0}=o_{p}(1)
and
|\sigma_{n}^{\rm cf,het}-\sigma_{n}^{\rm cf,het,0}|=o_{p}(1)
.
The latter is already established. For the former, we have, with
\mathcal{G}_{n}=\sigma(A_{1:n},X_{1:n},M_{1:n})
:

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[(L-L^{0})^{2}\mid\mathcal{G}_{n}]

\displaystyle=\frac{n}{4}\mathbb{E}\left[\left(\sum_{i\in\mathcal{D}_{2},A_{i}=1}(\hat{\tau}^{(2)}_{1,i}-\hat{\tau}^{(2),0}_{1,i}){\epsilon}_{i}+\sum_{i\in\mathcal{D}_{1},A_{i}=1}(\hat{\tau}^{(1)}_{1,i}-\hat{\tau}^{(1),0}_{1,i}){\epsilon}_{i}\right)^{2}\mid\mathcal{G}_{n}\right]

\displaystyle\leq\frac{nc_{+}}{4}\sum_{j=1}^{2}\|\hat{\tau}^{(j)}_{1}-\hat{\tau}^{(j),0}_{1}\|_{2}^{2}=o_{p}(1)\,.

where the last conclusion follows from Lemma
B.1
. Consequently, by conditional Markov’s inequality,
L-L^{0}=o_{p}(1)
. This completes the proof of asymptotic normality of
\hat{\theta}_{10}^{\rm cf}
around
\theta_{10}
.

Next, we will establish the asymptotic normality of
\hat{\theta}_{10}^{\rm cf}
around
\theta^{\rm pop}_{10}
. The proof here is closely related to that of Corollary
5.11
. We use the same notation and definition for
V_{n}^{\rm cf},V_{n}^{\rm cf,0},T_{n}^{\rm cf},A_{n},B_{n}
and
L_{n}^{\rm cf,0}
as used/introduced in the proof of Corollary
5.11
. Our first goal is to prove an analogous result of Lemma
B.2
with
\sigma_{n}^{\rm cf}
replaced by
\sigma_{n}^{\rm cf,het}
. The proof is almost exactly the same, the only thing we need to use is
(\sigma_{n}^{\rm cf,het,0}/\sigma_{n}^{\rm cf,het})-1=o_{p}(1)
along with the fact that
\sigma_{n}^{\rm cf,het}
and
\sigma_{n}^{\rm cf,het,0}
are bounded away from
0
with probabibility
1
as we have already argued in the previous part. Therefore, we also have:

\mathbb{P}\left(\frac{\sqrt{n}(\hat{\theta}_{10}^{\rm cf}-\theta_{10})}{\sigma_{n}^{\rm cf,het}}\leq t\mid\mathcal{X}_{n}\right)\longrightarrow\Phi(t),\hskip 9.24994pt\text{in probability with respect to }\mathcal{X}_{n}\,.

Then the exact same calculation as in Corollary
5.11
yields

\left|\mathbb{E}\left[{\sf exp}\left\{iu(A_{n}/\sigma_{n}^{\rm cf,het})+ivB_{n}\right\}\right]-e^{-u^{2}/2}\mathbb{E}[e^{ivB_{n}}]\right|\longrightarrow 0,

(B.15)

The last part is also a similar sub-sequence argument. Define
\omega_{n}^{\rm het}=(\sigma_{n}^{\rm cf,het})^{2}/((\sigma_{n}^{\rm cf,het})^{2}+\sigma^{2}_{X})
, where, as in Corollary
5.11

\sigma^{2}_{X}=\Lambda^{\top}{\sf var}(X)\Lambda
with
\Lambda=(\beta_{1}+\mathbf{B}_{0}^{\top}\gamma_{1})
. As per our assumption, there exists a deterministic sequence
\bar{\omega}_{n}^{\rm het}\in[0,1]
such that
\omega_{n}^{\rm het}-\bar{\omega}_{n}^{\rm het}=o_{p}(1)
. As
\sigma_{X}^{2}\leq C
(bounded), given any sequence, there exists a further subsequence such that along that subsequence
\bar{\omega}_{n}^{\rm het}\to\omega\in[0,1]
and
\sigma_{X}^{2}\to\zeta^{2}\geq 0
. Assume
\zeta>0
.
We use the same index
n
for this subsequence for notational simplicity.
Now, by sub-Gaussianity on the design,
\{(X_{i}-\mu_{X})^{\top}\Lambda:1\leq i\leq n\}
satisfies the Lindeberg condition, and consequently we have along this subsequence:

\textstyle B_{n}=\sqrt{n}(\bar{X}-\mu_{X})^{\top}\Lambda\xrightarrow{d}\mathcal{N}(0,\zeta^{2})\,.

This, along with Equation (
B.15
) implies:

\textstyle\left(\frac{A_{n}}{\sigma_{n}^{\rm cf,het}},\ B_{n}\right)\xrightarrow{d}(Z_{1},Z_{2}),\ \ Z_{1}\sim\mathcal{N}(0,1),Z_{2}\sim\mathcal{N}(0,\zeta^{2}),\ Z_{1}\perp\!\!\!\!\perp Z_{2}\,.

Define
D^{2}_{n}=(\sigma_{n}^{\rm cf,het})^{2}+\sigma^{2}_{X}
. Then
D_{n}=\sigma_{X}/\sqrt{1-\omega_{n}^{\rm het}}
. Then:

\textstyle(A_{n}+B_{n})/D_{n}=\sqrt{\omega_{n}^{\rm het}}(A_{n}/\sigma_{n}^{\rm cf,het})+(B_{n}/D_{n})

Therefore, along the subsequence, by Slutsky’s theorem, we have:

\textstyle\frac{A_{n}+B_{n}}{D_{n}}\xrightarrow{d}\sqrt{\omega}Z_{1}+\frac{\sqrt{1-\omega}}{\zeta}Z_{2}\sim\mathcal{N}(0,1)\,.

The case
\zeta=0
is handled exactly in the same way as in the proof of Corollary
5.11
and hence is skipped. Therefore, we have concluded that given any sequence, there exists a further sub-sequence such that along that sub-sequence
(A_{n}+B_{n})/D_{n}\xrightarrow{d}\mathcal{N}(0,1)
. As the limiting distribution is the same along all such subsequences, we conclude that the entire sequence
(A_{n}+B_{n})/D_{n}
converges to
\mathcal{N}(0,1)
. This completes the proof.
∎

B.5
Proof of Theorem
5.13

Proof.

Recall from the definition of NDE, we have
\hat{\theta}_{\rm NDE}^{\rm cf}=\hat{\theta}_{10}^{\rm cf}-\hat{\theta}_{00}^{\rm cf}
, where

\textstyle\hat{\theta}_{00}^{\rm cf}=\frac{\hat{\theta}_{00}^{(1)}+\hat{\theta}_{00}^{(2)}}{2}=\frac{1}{2}\sum_{j=1}^{2}\left(\bar{X}^{\top}\hat{\rho}_{c}^{(j)}+(\hat{\tau}_{2}^{(j)})^{\top}(\mathbf{y}_{c,j}-\mathbf{X}_{c,j}\hat{\rho}_{c}^{(j)})\right)

From the reduced form regression we have:

\displaystyle Y

\displaystyle=A(X^{\top}\beta_{1}+M^{\top}\gamma_{1}+{\epsilon})+(1-A)(X^{\top}\beta_{0}+M^{\top}\gamma_{0}+{\epsilon}^{\prime})

\displaystyle=A(X^{\top}\beta_{1}+X^{\top}\mathbf{B}_{1}^{\top}\gamma_{1}+\gamma_{1}^{\top}U^{\prime}+{\epsilon})+(1-A)(X^{\top}\beta_{0}+X^{\top}\mathbf{B}_{0}^{\top}\gamma_{0}+\gamma_{0}^{\top}U+{\epsilon}^{\prime})

Therefore, on the control arm,
Y=X^{\top}\rho_{c}+\xi_{0}
, where
\rho_{c}=\beta_{0}+\mathbf{B}_{0}^{\top}\gamma_{0}
and
\xi_{0}=U^{\top}\gamma_{0}+{\epsilon}^{\prime}
. Using this we can decompose the estimation error of
\hat{\theta}_{00}^{\rm cf}
as:

\displaystyle\hat{\theta}_{00}^{\rm cf}-\theta_{00}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\sum_{j=1}^{2}\left(\bar{X}^{\top}(\hat{\rho}_{c}^{(j)}-\rho_{c})+(\hat{\tau}_{2}^{(j)})^{\top}(\mathbf{y}_{c,j}-\mathbf{X}_{c,j}\hat{\rho}_{c}^{(j)})\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\sum_{j=1}^{2}(\bar{X}-\mathbf{X}_{c,j}^{\top}\hat{\tau}_{2}^{(j)})^{\top}(\hat{\rho}_{c}^{(j)}-\rho_{c})+\frac{1}{2}\sum_{j=1}^{2}(\hat{\tau}_{2}^{(j)})^{\top}{\bm{\xi}}_{0}:=\Delta_{n,00}^{\rm cf}+V_{n,00}^{\rm cf}

This, along with the decomposition of
\hat{\theta}_{\rm 10}^{\rm cf}
(Equation (
B.2
)) yields:

\frac{\sqrt{n}(\hat{\theta}_{\rm NDE}^{\rm cf}-\theta_{\rm NDE})}{\sigma_{n,\rm NDE}^{\rm cf,het}}=\frac{\sqrt{n}(\Delta_{n}^{\rm cf}-\Delta_{n,00}^{\rm cf}+V_{n}^{\rm cf}-V_{n,00}^{\rm cf})}{\sigma_{n,\rm NDE}^{\rm cf,het}}

The proof proceeds similarly to that of Theorem
5.9
. We first show
\sqrt{n}(\Delta_{n}^{\rm cf}-\Delta_{n,00}^{\rm cf})=o_{p}(1)
, then we show
\sigma_{n,\rm NDE}^{\rm cf,het}
is bounded away from
0
with probability going to
1
, and finally we establish that
\sqrt{n}(V_{n}^{\rm cf}-V_{n,00}^{\rm cf})/\sigma_{n,\rm NDE}^{\rm cf,het}
converges to
\mathcal{N}(0,1)
.
We have already established in the proof of Theorem
5.9
that
\sqrt{n}\Delta_{n}^{\rm cf}=o_{p}(1)
. Here, we will first show that
\sqrt{n}\Delta_{n,00}^{\rm cf}=o_{p}(1)
. Given that
\|\rho_{c}\|_{0}=\|\beta_{0}+\mathbf{B}_{0}^{\top}\gamma_{0}\|_{0}\leq k_{\beta}+sk_{\gamma}\leq(1+s)k
and the error
\xi_{0}
satisfies
\mathbb{E}[\xi_{0}\mid X,A=0]=0
and sub-Gaussian (uniformly over
X
, Assumption
5.3
) and the design matrix satisfies RE condition with probability going to
1
, the lasso estimator
\hat{\rho}_{c}^{(j)}
satisfies by same argument in Theorem
5.9

\textstyle\|\hat{\rho}_{c}^{(j)}-\rho_{c}\|_{1}=O_{p}\left(sk\sqrt{\frac{\log{p}}{n}}\right)\,,\ \|\hat{\rho}_{c}^{(j)}-\rho_{c}\|_{2}=O_{p}\left(\sqrt{\frac{sk\log{p}}{n}}\right)\,.

As a consequence:

\displaystyle\sqrt{n}|\Delta_{n,00}^{\rm cf}|

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{2}\sum_{j=1}^{2}\|\bar{X}-\mathbf{X}_{c,j}^{\top}\hat{\tau}_{2}^{(j)}\|_{\infty}\|\hat{\rho}_{c}^{(j)}-\rho_{c}\|_{1}

\displaystyle=O_{p}\left(\sqrt{n}\times\sqrt{\frac{\log{p}}{n}}\times sk\sqrt{\frac{\log{p}}{n}}\right)=O_{p}\left(\frac{sk\log{p}}{\sqrt{n}}\right)=o_{p}(1)

where the last conclusion follows from Assumption
5.4
. Here we have also used the fact that
\|\bar{X}-\mathbf{X}_{c}^{\top}\hat{\tau}^{(j)}_{2}\|_{\infty}\leq K_{2}\sqrt{\log{p}/n}
which is by definition of the optimization problem (
3.1
).
This completes the proof of
\sqrt{n}(\Delta_{n}^{\rm cf}-\Delta_{n,00}^{\rm cf})=o_{p}(1)
.

We next argue that
\sigma_{n,\rm NDE}^{\rm cf,het}
is bounded away from
0
with probability going to
1
. The proof is very similar to that of the second step of Theorem
5.9
; from the definition, we have:

\displaystyle(\sigma_{n,\rm NDE}^{\rm cf,het})^{2}

\displaystyle=\frac{n}{4}\sum_{j=1}^{2}\left[\sum_{i\in\mathcal{D}_{j},A_{i}=1}(\hat{\tau}_{1,i}^{(j)})^{2}\nu_{1}(X_{i},M_{i})+\sum_{i\in\mathcal{D}_{j},A_{i}=0}(\hat{\tau}_{2,i}^{(j)})^{2}\omega_{0}(X_{i})\right]

\displaystyle\geq\frac{c_{-}}{4}\ n\|\hat{\tau}^{(1)}_{1}\|_{2}^{2}

Here we have used the fact that
\nu_{1}(x,m)\geq c_{-}
(Assumption
5.3
).
Now, we have already established in the proof of Theorem
5.9

n\|\hat{\tau}^{(1)}_{1}\|_{2}^{2}
is bounded away from
0
with probability going to
1
. This immediately implies
\sigma_{n,\rm NDE}^{\rm cf,het}
is bounded away from
0
with probability going to
1
.

The final part is to establish the asymptotic normality. Similar to the proof of Theorem
5.9
. From the definition of
V_{n}^{\rm cf}
and
V_{n,00}^{\rm cf}
, we have:
